\title{Triple quantum Schubert calculus}
\author{Matthew J. Samuel}

\begin{document}

\begin{abstract}
We prove a triple quantum Pieri formula: the product of a quantum double Schubert polynomial $\qsch_u(x;y)$ by a quantum factorial elementary polynomial $\qelem_{p,k}(x;z)$ in a second set of variables $z$ is a sum over chains in the quantum Bruhat graph, indexed by a quantum analogue of Sottile's relation, whose coefficients are monomials in the quantum parameters times polynomials in the differences $y_i-z_j$ with nonnegative integer coefficients, and which is Graham-positive at $z=y$. At $y=z=0$ it is the quantum Pieri rule of Ciocan-Fontanine and Postnikov; at $z=y$ it is an equivariant quantum Pieri rule for the complete flag variety, indexed by chains in the quantum Bruhat graph rather than, as in the rule of Huang and Li, by the data of a reduction to classical cohomology, and proved directly. The mixed-variable form is what makes the formula a tool: it is the quantum analogue of the classical triple Pieri formula that drives the package \texttt{schubmult}. A doubled Pieri formula, positive in the same senses, treats multiplication by the quantization of a product of two elementary polynomials in the same variables, which is not the product of the quantizations: it is two iterated Pieri steps with the incompatible pairs of chains omitted. More generally, the triple quantum Schubert structure constants $c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;z)$, the coefficients of $\qsch_u(x;y)\qsch_v(x;z)$ in the basis $\{\qsch_w(x;y)\}$, are polynomials in the $y_i-z_j$ with nonnegative integer coefficients, by a theorem of Xiao and Yue whose proof appears as an appendix. We extend the Peterson--Woodward comparison formula and the quantum-to-classical reduction of Leung--Li and Huang--Li to the triple constants, and we describe and prove correct the algorithms of \texttt{schubmult}, which compute products of arbitrary quantum double Schubert polynomials well beyond the reach of previous methods.
\end{abstract}
\maketitle

{\bf Keywords}: quantum double Schubert polynomial, structure constant, Littlewood-Richardson rule, Pieri formula, quantum Bruhat graph, Peterson--Woodward comparison formula

{\bf Mathematics classification codes}: 05E05, 05A05, 05A15, 14N10

{\bf Email}: matthematics@gmail.com

\section{Introduction}

Let $\sch_w(x;y)$, $w\in S_\infty$, denote the double Schubert polynomials of Lascoux and Sch\"utzenberger, which represent the torus-equivariant Schubert classes of the complete flag variety, and let $\qsch_w(x;y)$ denote the quantum double Schubert polynomials of Kirillov and Maeno \cite{kirillov2000quantum}, which represent the Schubert classes in torus-equivariant quantum cohomology \cite{lam2014quantum}. The structure constants of the equivariant quantum cohomology ring are the coefficients in
$$\qsch_u(x;y)\qsch_v(x;y)=\sum_{w,d}q^d\,c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;y)\,\qsch_w(x;y),$$
and Mihalcea \cite{mihalcea2006positivity} proved that they are polynomials in the negative simple roots $y_{i+1}-y_i$ with nonnegative integer coefficients.

In this paper we study the coefficients obtained when the two factors are polynomials in different sets of variables, $y$ and $z$:
$$\qsch_u(x;y)\qsch_v(x;z)=\sum_{w,d}q^d\,c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;z)\,\qsch_w(x;y).$$
We call the $c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;z)$ the \emph{triple quantum Schubert structure constants}. At $z=y$ they recover the equivariant quantum structure constants, and at $q=0$ they recover the triple Schubert structure constants $c_{u,v}^w(y;z)$ of \cite{samuelmolev}, which were recently shown by Gao and Xiong \cite{gao2025grahampositivitytripleschubert} to be polynomials in the differences $y_i-z_j$ with nonnegative integer coefficients. The triple constants carry strictly more information than the equivariant ones: the equivariant quantum constant $c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;y)$ is a specialization of $c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;z)$, while conversely $c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;z)$ is recovered from the constants $c_{u,v_2}^{w,d}(y;y)$, $v_2$ ranging over the right factors of the reduced factorizations $v=v_1\cdot v_2$, by the quantum Cauchy identity (Lemma \ref{lemma:quantumtoordinary}).

The main results of this paper are the following; the positivity theorem is due to Xiao and Yue, the rest are ours.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{A triple quantum Pieri formula} (Theorem \ref{theorem:pieri}; general degree in Theorem \ref{theorem:pierigeneral}). Let $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)=\qsch_{s_1s_2\cdots s_k}(x;z)$ be the quantum factorial elementary polynomial. We prove
$$\qsch_u(x;y)\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)=\sum_{u\tom{k} w}q_k(u,w)\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,w)}(y_a-z_1)\,\qsch_w(x;y),$$
where $\tom{k}$ is a quantum analogue of the relation introduced by Sottile \cite{sottile} for the classical Pieri rule, $q_k(u,w)$ is a monomial in the $q_i$ recording the length-decreasing steps of a chain from $u$ to $w$ in the quantum Bruhat graph, and $\fx_k(u,w)$ is the set of values fixed by the chain in the first $k$ positions. The general-degree version expands $\qsch_u(x;y)\qelem_{p,k}(x;z)$ for every $p\leq k$ over the same chains, the coefficient of $\qsch_w(x;y)$ being $q_k(u,w)$ times a factorial elementary polynomial in the variables $y_a$, $a\in\fx_k(u,w)$, and $z$. In either form the coefficient of each $q$-monomial is a polynomial in the differences $y_i-z_j$ with nonnegative integer coefficients. This is the main result of the paper.

The word triple is essential. Mixed-variable formulas of this kind go back to the Littlewood--Richardson rule of Molev and Sagan for factorial Schur functions \cite{molev1999littlewood}, and the classical triple Pieri formula \cite[Theorem~7.1]{samuelmolev}, to which the present one reduces at $q=0$, has already shown what such a formula is good for: it is the engine of the multiplication algorithm of \texttt{schubmult} for double Schubert polynomials. The reason is that a formula with $z\neq y$ composes: an arbitrary $\qsch_v(x;z)$ expands into products of factorial elementary polynomials in the $z$-variables alone (Theorems \ref{theorem:quandouble} and \ref{theorem:quandoublemed}), and the triple Pieri formula then multiplies each factor against $\qsch_u(x;y)$ without any further change of variables. An equivariant formula, with $z=y$, cannot be iterated in this way. Conversely the triple formula determines the equivariant one by specialization, while recovering it from equivariant data requires the quantum Cauchy identity and a sum over factorizations (Lemma \ref{lemma:quantumtoordinary}).

The specializations locate the formula among known results. At $q=0$ it is the triple Pieri formula of \cite{samuelmolev}, and at $q=0$, $y=z=0$ it is Sottile's rule. At $y=z=0$ it is the quantum Pieri formula for complete flag manifolds, due to Ciocan-Fontanine \cite{ciocan1999partial} and reformulated by Postnikov \cite{postnikov1999quantum} in terms of what is now called the quantum Bruhat graph; the relation $\tom{k}$ is the factorial version of Postnikov's. At $p=1$ and $z=y$ it is the equivariant quantum Chevalley (Monk) formula of Mihalcea \cite{mihalcea2007equivariant}. At $z=y$ and general $p$ it is an equivariant quantum Pieri rule for the complete flag variety. Such a rule was first obtained by Huang and Li \cite[Theorem~3.10]{huang2015equivariant}, for all partial flag varieties of type A, by applying the quantum-to-classical reduction repeatedly until no quantum parameters remain and then invoking Robinson's equivariant Pieri rule \cite{robinsonpieri}; its terms are indexed by the data of Ciocan-Fontanine's rule, namely a unimodal degree $d$, the permutations $\tau_d$ and $\phi_d$ it determines, and classical chains from $u\tau_d$ to $w\phi_d$, and its coefficients are restrictions of special Schubert classes of Grassmannians to torus-fixed points. Our formula differs from theirs in three respects. It has independent sets of variables $y$ and $z$, which is what makes it composable. Its terms are indexed intrinsically, by chains in the quantum Bruhat graph starting at $u$, with the quantum degree $q_k(u,w)$ and the weight $\fx_k(u,w)$ read off from the chain, so that it stands to the formula of Huang and Li as Postnikov's formulation of the non-equivariant rule stands to Ciocan-Fontanine's. And its proof is a direct analysis of the quantum Bruhat graph rather than a reduction.

It is worth separating the kinds of positivity in play. Positivity of the coefficients follows from Mihalcea's theorem at $z=y$ and from Theorem \ref{theorem:positivity} in general, without any formula; triple positivity does not by itself imply Graham positivity at $z=y$, but the Pieri coefficients are specializations of factorial elementary polynomials and are manifestly Graham-positive there (Corollary \ref{corollary:pierigraham}), exactly as in \cite{samuelmolev}. The quantum-to-classical reduction of Leung--Li \cite{leung2012classical} and Huang--Li \cite{huang2015equivariant} gives more: whenever one factor is Grassmannian, each structure constant equals an explicitly determined classical one, a statement we show holds verbatim for the triple constants (Proposition \ref{prop:grassmannianreduction}), and combining this with a positive classical rule gives a positive expression for each coefficient separately, which is how Huang and Li obtain their formula. A rule indexed by chains from $u$ is a different kind of statement: it describes the support of the product, the degrees $d$ and the permutations $w$ that occur, together with all coefficients, without reference to a target $(w,d)$, and it is this that the multiplication algorithm of Section \ref{section:algorithms} iterates. The proof occupies Section \ref{section:proofpieri}; it proceeds by induction on $k$ via the single-variable Monk formula, and rests on an intrinsic description of the chains (every nontrivial cycle of $u^{-1}w$ has exactly one element exceeding $k$, and a single length identity) which makes the term-by-term cancellations tractable.
\item \emph{A doubled quantum Pieri formula} (Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri}). The quantization map of Fomin, Gelfand and Postnikov is not multiplicative on products of elementary polynomials in the same number of variables, so the quantum Schubert polynomial of a $k\times2$ rectangle is not the product of two quantum factorial elementary polynomials. We show that the product $\qsch_u(x;y)\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)$, where $d_2[k]$ is the dominant permutation whose diagram is the $k\times2$ rectangle, is nevertheless given by two iterated Pieri steps, provided one omits the pairs of chains that are not \emph{compatible} in a precise cycle-theoretic sense (Definition \ref{def:compatible}). The doubled rule is equivalent to a termwise identity between pairs of chains (Theorem \ref{theorem:termwise}) which telescopes along explicit blocks of pairs and is then established by an injection built from a single local lemma, a generalization of the up/down pairing used in the proof of the Pieri formula (Theorem \ref{theorem:junction}). By quantum Giambelli the same product is an alternating sum of iterated Pieri steps; the doubled rule is the cancellation-free form of that sum, and it is the ``paired step'' of the multiplication algorithm.
\item \emph{Positivity} (Theorem \ref{theorem:positivity}). The constants $c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;z)$ lie in $\mathbb Z_{\geq0}[y_i-z_j]$. This theorem and its proof are due to Tongren Xiao and Yihua Yue \cite{xiaoyuequantumpositivity}, who kindly agreed to include the proof as Appendix \ref{appendix:positivity}; it combines the geometric argument of Mihalcea with the Graham-positivity theorem of Gao and Xiong for $B^-$-stable effective cycles. We include it because it settles what would otherwise be a conjecture about the constants studied here, of which the Pieri formulas above are explicit combinatorial instances.
\item \emph{Parabolic and quantum-to-classical reductions} (Proposition \ref{prop:parabolic}, Theorem \ref{theorem:quantclassz}). The Peterson--Woodward comparison formula \cite{woodward2005d}, in the form proved by Huang and Li \cite{huang2015equivariant} for equivariant quantum cohomology, extends verbatim to the triple constants. Consequently the triple structure constants of the parabolic quantum double Schubert polynomials of Lam and Shimozono are equal to triple constants of the complete flag variety, and in particular they are also Graham-positive (Corollary \ref{corollary:parabolicpositivity}). The quantum-to-classical reduction of Leung--Li and Huang--Li extends to the triple constants as well. For Grassmannians, considerably more is known at $z=y$: Mihalcea \cite{mihalcea2006equivariant} gave an equivariant quantum Pieri rule and a recursive characterization of the equivariant quantum structure constants, and Buch's mutation algorithm for puzzles \cite{buch2015mutations}, combined with the equivariant ``quantum equals classical'' theorem of Buch and Mihalcea \cite{buch2011qkgrass}, provides a Graham-positive combinatorial Littlewood--Richardson rule for $QH_T^*(\mathrm{Gr}(k,n))$. No such rule is known to us for the triple constants with $z\neq y$, even in the Grassmannian case.
\item \emph{Algorithms} (Section \ref{section:algorithms}). Computing products of Schubert classes in $QH^*(Fl_n)$, even non-equivariantly, is difficult in practice: the general methods available, namely reduction of quantum Giambelli determinants modulo the quantum ideal and iteration of the quantum Monk or Chevalley formula with a recursive reduction, become infeasible well before $n$ reaches the range in which interesting phenomena appear. We describe the algorithms implemented in the package \texttt{schubmult} \cite{schubmult}, which compute products of arbitrary (quantum) double Schubert polynomials, with independent parameters $y$ and $z$, by combining the Pieri formulas on one factor with a signed expansion of the other factor into products of factorial elementary polynomials. In our experience this is by a wide margin the fastest method available, and it is the only one we know of that handles the triple constants directly. We prove the expansion, in its classical form (Theorem \ref{theorem:orddouble}) and in two quantum forms, over a strict dominant shape (Theorem \ref{theorem:quandouble}) and over the ``medium'' shape actually used by the implementation, in which a pair of equal columns is allowed when it is preceded by a drop of at least two and contributes a single doubled factor (Theorem \ref{theorem:quandoublemed}); the key step is a formula for the skew divided differences, in the sense of \cite{samuelleibniz,samuelmolev}, of a product $\prod_{j\leq k}(x_j-z_i)$ with respect to the $z$-variables (Theorem \ref{theorem:skewtop}), in which the terms of the iterated Monk rule that revisit a position cancel by a rotation argument (Lemma \ref{lemma:rotation}). The doubled Pieri rule is exactly what is needed to process the equal columns of the medium shape.
\end{enumerate}

The paper is organized as follows. Section \ref{section:definitions} defines the triple constants, states the positivity theorem and treats the parabolic case. Section \ref{section:pieri} states the Pieri formula, whose proof is given in Section \ref{section:proofpieri}. Section \ref{section:algorithms} states the expansion formulas for (quantum) double Schubert polynomials and describes the multiplication algorithms built on them; the expansion formulas are proved in Section \ref{section:proofvside}. Section \ref{section:quantclass} treats the quantum-to-classical reduction, Section \ref{section:additional} the general-degree and doubled Pieri formulas, and Section \ref{section:pw} proves the parabolic reduction via the Peterson--Woodward comparison formula. Appendix \ref{appendix:positivity}, by Xiao and Yue, contains the proof of the positivity theorem.

\textbf{Acknowledgements.} We thank Tongren Xiao and Yihua Yue for contributing Appendix \ref{appendix:positivity}, their proof of Theorem \ref{theorem:positivity}, and Rui Xiong and Changzheng Li for making us aware of their work.

\section{Triple quantum Schubert structure constants} \label{section:definitions}

\subsection{Quantum double Schubert polynomials and the triple constants}
We fix $n\geq 1$ and write
$$x=(x_1,\ldots,x_n),\qquad y=(y_1,\ldots,y_n),\qquad q=(q_1,\ldots,q_{n-1}).$$
For $1\leq a<b\leq n$, set
$$q_{ab}:=q_aq_{a+1}\cdots q_{b-1},$$
and use the cohomological grading $\deg q_i=2$ (equivalently $\deg q_{ab}=2(b-a)$).

Let $s_i$ act on the $y$-variables by swapping $y_i,y_{i+1}$, and define the divided difference operators
$$\partial_i^{(y)}f:=\frac{f-s_i f}{y_i-y_{i+1}}\qquad(1\leq i\leq n-1).$$
For $w\in S_n$, if $w=s_{i_1}\cdots s_{i_r}$ is any reduced word, set
$$\partial_w^{(y)}:=\partial_{i_1}^{(y)}\cdots \partial_{i_r}^{(y)}.$$

Following Kirillov--Maeno \cite{kirillov2000quantum}, define the quantum elementary polynomials $e_j^{(q)}(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$ by
$$\Delta_k(t\mid x_1,\ldots,x_k):=\sum_{j=0}^k t^{k-j}e_j^{(q)}(x_1,\ldots,x_k),$$
where
$$\Delta_k(t\mid x_1,\ldots,x_k)=\det\!\begin{pmatrix}
x_1+t & q_1 & 0 & \cdots & 0\\
-1 & x_2+t & q_2 & \ddots & \vdots\\
0 & -1 & x_3+t & \ddots & 0\\
\vdots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & q_{k-1}\\
0 & \cdots & 0 & -1 & x_k+t
\end{pmatrix}.
$$

Let $w_0\in S_n$ be the longest permutation. The top quantum double Schubert polynomial is
$$\qsch_{w_0}(x;y):=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\Delta_i(-y_{n-i}\mid x_1,\ldots,x_i),$$
and for $w\in S_n$ we define
$$\qsch_w(x;y):=(-1)^{\ell(w_0) - \ell(w)}\partial_{ww_0}^{(y)}\qsch_{w_0}(x;y).$$
Its specialization
$$\qsch_w(x):=\qsch_w(x;0)$$
is the quantum Schubert polynomial. In particular, at $q=0$ these recover the ordinary double and single Schubert polynomials.

\subsubsection*{The stable setting}
The polynomials $\qsch_w(x;y)$ are stable \cite{kirillov2000quantum}: for $w\in S_n$, $\qsch_w(x;y)$ involves only $x_1,\ldots,x_{n-1}$, $y_1,\ldots,y_{n-1}$ and $q_1,\ldots,q_{n-2}$, and it does not change when $w$ is regarded as an element of $S_{n+1}$ under the standard inclusion $S_n\subset S_{n+1}$. (This is also immediate from the description $\qsch_w(x;y)=\mathcal Q(\sch_w(x;y))$ in the proof of Lemma \ref{lemma:quantumtoordinary} below, since the quantization map $\mathcal Q$ does not depend on $n$.) From now on we therefore let the number of variables be infinite,
$$x=(x_1,x_2,\ldots),\qquad y=(y_1,y_2,\ldots),\qquad z=(z_1,z_2,\ldots),\qquad q=(q_1,q_2,\ldots),$$
index the polynomials by $S_\infty=\bigcup_nS_n$, and work in the polynomial ring $\mathbb Z[q][x,y,z]$ in these (countably many) variables, where $\mathbb Z[q]=\mathbb Z[q_1,q_2,\ldots]$. A \emph{degree} is an element $d=(d_1,d_2,\ldots)$ of $\bigoplus_{i\geq1}\mathbb Z_{\geq0}$, i.e.\ a weak composition with finitely many nonzero parts, and $q^d=\prod_iq_i^{d_i}$; in Section \ref{section:definitions:parabolic} degrees will be regarded as effective elements of the coroot lattice. As a module over $\mathbb{Z}[q][y]$, the quantum double Schubert polynomials $\qsch_w(x;y)$, $w\in S_\infty$, form a basis of $\mathbb{Z}[q][x,y]$: the quantization map $\mathcal Q$ of Lemma \ref{lemma:quantumtoordinary} is a $\mathbb Z[q][y]$-linear bijection of $\mathbb Z[q][x,y]$ onto itself, since it preserves the degree, is the identity modulo $q$, and maps each finite-rank $\mathbb Z$-module of homogeneous elements of fixed degree in $q_1,\ldots,q_{m-1}$, $x_1,\ldots,x_m$, $y_1,\ldots,y_m$ into itself, and it carries the basis $\{\sch_w(x;y)\}$ to $\{\qsch_w(x;y)\}$. We may therefore write
$$\qsch_u(x;y)\qsch_v(x;z)=\sum_{w,d}q^dc_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;z)\qsch_w(x;y),$$
the sum being over $w\in S_\infty$ and all degrees $d$, with only finitely many nonzero terms. The coefficients $c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;z)$ are the \emph{triple quantum Schubert structure constants}, which are polynomials in $y$ and $z$ with integer coefficients.

The finite-dimensional meaning of this expansion is the following. Given $u,v\in S_n$, choose $N$ so large that every $w$ occurring in the expansion lies in $S_N$ and every occurring $d$ has $d_i=0$ for $i\geq N$; this holds for all sufficiently large $N$, since there are finitely many terms. Then the expansion is an identity in $\mathbb Z[q_1,\ldots,q_{N-1}][x_1,\ldots,x_N,y,z]$, and reducing it modulo the ideal of Kim's presentation of $QH_T^*(\mathrm{Fl}_N)$ \cite{kim1996equivariant} (the ideal $J_{qy}^N$ recalled in Appendix \ref{appendix:positivity}) identifies it, by \cite{lam2014quantum}, with the expansion of the product of the two corresponding classes in the Schubert basis of $QH_T^*(\mathrm{Fl}_N)$, with $z=y$. Thus $c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;y)$ is the equivariant quantum Littlewood--Richardson coefficient of $\mathrm{Fl}_N$ for every such $N$, and the degree vector $d$ is the degree $\sum_id_i\alpha_i^\vee$ of a rational curve in $\mathrm{Fl}_N$. Note that $w\notin S_n$ and $d_i\neq0$ for $i\geq n-1$ do occur: for instance $\qsch_{21}(x;y)^2=(y_2-y_1)\qsch_{21}(x;y)+\qsch_{231}(x;y)+q_1$, the last term coming from the quantum Monk formula in $\mathrm{Fl}_N$ for any $N\geq3$.

The product respects the grading where for each $i$, $\deg x_i=\deg y_i=\deg z_i=1$ and $\deg q_i=2$, in which $\qsch_w$ is homogeneous of degree $\ell(w)$. In particular, if $c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;z)\neq 0$, then
$$\deg c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;z)=\ell(u)+\ell(v)-\ell(w)-2\sum_i d_i;$$
for instance the term $q_1$ in $\qsch_{21}^2$ above has a coefficient of degree $0=1+1-0-2$. Unlike the usual triple Schubert structure constants, they do not exhibit triangularity in Bruhat order, and indeed we may have $\ell(w) < \ell(u) + \ell(v)$. There is an ordering in which the quantum double Schubert polynomials are triangular, but we will not need this in full generality.

Throughout, a factorization $w=v_1\cdot v_2$ written with a dot is understood to be \emph{reduced}, i.e.\ $\ell(w)=\ell(v_1)+\ell(v_2)$. The following identity will be used repeatedly to pass between the triple constants and the equivariant quantum structure constants $c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;y)$. Setting $y=0$ in it recovers the expansion $\qsch_w(x;z)=\sum_{v_1\cdot v_2=w}\sch_{v_1}(0;z)\qsch_{v_2}(x)$ of Kirillov--Maeno \cite{kirillov2000quantum} in terms of quantum Schubert polynomials.

\begin{lemma}[Quantum Cauchy identity] \label{lemma:quantumtoordinary}
For all $w\in S_\infty$,
$$\qsch_w(x;z)=\sum_{v_1\cdot v_2=w}\sch_{v_1}(y;z)\,\qsch_{v_2}(x;y).$$
Consequently, for all $u,v,w$ and $d$,
$$c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;z)=\sum_{v_1\cdot v_2=v}c_{u,v_2}^{w,d}(y;y)\,\sch_{v_1}(y;z).$$
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $\mathcal Q$ denote the quantization map of Fomin, Gelfand and Postnikov \cite{fomin1997quantum}, used throughout the paper: the $\mathbb Z[q]$-linear map on polynomials in $x$ which sends each standard elementary monomial $\prod_j e_{i_j}(x_1,\ldots,x_j)$ to $\prod_j e^{(q)}_{i_j}(x_1,\ldots,x_j)$, extended linearly over any further coefficient variables. Since the divided differences in the $y$-variables commute with $\mathcal Q$, the definition of $\qsch_w(x;y)$ by $y$-divided differences gives $\qsch_w(x;y)=\mathcal Q(\sch_w(x;y))$ \cite[(10)]{lam2014quantum}. Now apply $\mathcal Q$, which is $\mathbb Z[y,z]$-linear, to the classical Cauchy-type expansion $\sch_w(x;z)=\sum_{v_1\cdot v_2=w}\sch_{v_1}(y;z)\sch_{v_2}(x;y)$ \cite[(6.3)]{notes}: the left side becomes $\qsch_w(x;z)$ and the right side becomes $\sum_{v_1\cdot v_2=w}\sch_{v_1}(y;z)\qsch_{v_2}(x;y)$. For the second assertion, multiply the first identity (with $w=v$) by $\qsch_u(x;y)$ and expand each $\qsch_u(x;y)\qsch_{v_2}(x;y)$ in the basis $\{\qsch_w(x;y)\}$ with coefficients $q^dc_{u,v_2}^{w,d}(y;y)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[$\tom{k}$ and the quantum $k$-Bruhat order] \label{remark:qkbruhat}
The \emph{quantum $k$-Bruhat order} was introduced by Benedetti, Bergeron, Colmenarejo, Saliola, and Sottile \cite{benedetti2025quantum} and studied further by Colmenarejo and Mayers \cite{colmenarejo2026quantum}. That order $\preceq_k^{\mathbf q}$ on $S_\infty[\mathbf q]$ is generated by cover relations
$$u'\;\lessdot_k^{\mathbf q}\;\mathbf q^{\gamma}u't_{ab},\qquad a\leq k<b,$$
each of exactly two kinds: a \emph{classical} cover, with $\gamma=0$ and $\ell(u't_{ab})=\ell(u')+1$, or a \emph{quantum} cover, with $\ell(u')+1=\ell(\mathbf q^{\gamma}u't_{ab})=\ell(u't_{ab})+2(b-a)$, that is $\ell(u't_{ab})=\ell(u')-2(b-a)+1$ and $\gamma=e_a+\cdots+e_{b-1}$. 
These are exactly the two alternatives of condition (4) of Definition \ref{definition:pieri}. Consequently every sequence $t_{a_1b_1},\ldots,t_{a_pb_p}$ witnessing $u\tom{k}w$ is a saturated chain from $u$ to $\mathbf q^{\alpha}w$ in the quantum $k$-Bruhat order, where $\mathbf q^{\alpha}$ is the monomial recorded by $q_k(u,w)$; in particular $u\tom{k}w$ implies $u\preceq_k^{\mathbf q}\mathbf q^{\alpha}w$.

The relation $\tom{k}$ is \emph{not} the full order $\preceq_k^{\mathbf q}$, however. Definition \ref{definition:pieri} imposes the additional requirements that the lower indices $a_1,\ldots,a_p$ be pairwise distinct, so that $\tom{k}$ admits only those saturated chains of a special form. Thus $\tom{k}$ is strictly larger than the covering relation $\lessdot_k^{\mathbf q}$ but strictly smaller than $\preceq_k^{\mathbf q}$: a general chain in the quantum $k$-Bruhat order may reuse a lower index $a_i$, and such chains do not contribute to the Pieri expansion. This is the quantum counterpart of the classical situation, where the Pieri relation of \cite{sottile,samuelmolev} is likewise the restriction of the $k$-Bruhat order to chains with distinct lower indices, and it is exactly this restriction that makes the Pieri coefficients well defined.

What we use below is only that each individual step of a $\tom{k}$ chain is a cover, and hence is governed by the following length-free description of the covers \cite[Proposition~3.1]{benedetti2025quantum} (see also \cite[Proposition~4.2]{colmenarejo2026quantum}): with $i\leq k<j$, the step $u'\lessdot_k^{\mathbf q}\mathbf q^{\gamma}u't_{ij}$ is a classical cover exactly when $u'(i)<u'(j)$ and no $l$ with $i<l<j$ satisfies $u'(i)<u'(l)<u'(j)$, and it is a quantum cover exactly when $u'(i)>u'(j)$ and every $l$ with $i<l<j$ satisfies $u'(j)<u'(l)<u'(i)$. This characterization is an immediate consequence of the standard formula for the length of $u't_{ij}$ (Lemma \ref{lemma:lengthchange} below), and it is the form in which the covers are used throughout Section \ref{section:proofpieri}.
\end{remark}


\subsection{Positivity}

For $d=(0,\ldots,0)$, this is the ordinary triple Schubert calculus structure constant, and it was proved in \cite{gao2025grahampositivitytripleschubert} that these have the positivity property that they are polynomials in the differences $y_i-z_j$ with nonnegative integer coefficients. We will refer to this as \emph{triple Schubert positivity}. The following theorem of Xiao and Yue \cite{xiaoyuequantumpositivity} shows that it persists in the quantum setting.
\begin{theorem}[Xiao--Yue; triple quantum Schubert positivity] \label{theorem:positivity}
For all $u,v,w$ and $d$, the structure constants $c_{u,v}^{w,d}(y;z)$ are polynomials in the differences $y_i-z_j$ with nonnegative integer coefficients.
\end{theorem}
The proof, due to Tongren Xiao and Yihua Yue \cite{xiaoyuequantumpositivity}, is given in Appendix \ref{appendix:positivity}.


\subsection{The parabolic case} \label{section:definitions:parabolic}

Unlike ordinary cohomology or equivariant cohomology, quantum cohomology and equivariant quantum cohomology do not demonstrate simple functoriality, in the sense that a projection of the complete flag variety $G/B$ to a partial flag variety $G/P$ does not induce a homomorphism of rings $QH_T^*(G/P)\to QH_T^*(G/B)$, and indeed the quantum cohomology of $G/P$ is not isomorphic to the subring of the quantum cohomology of $G/B$ generated by the Schubert classes indexed by $W^P$. This is reflected in the fact that there are parabolic quantum double Schubert polynomials defined in a different manner that represent Schubert classes in $QH_T^*(G/P)$ \cite{lam2014quantum} and are not in general equal to the quantum double Schubert polynomials above. (The non-equivariant ring $QH^*(SL_n/P)$ itself is well understood: its presentation and its quantum Pieri and Giambelli formulas were proved by Ciocan-Fontanine \cite{ciocan1999partial} and, by elementary geometric arguments, by Buch \cite{buch2005partial}.)

\begin{definition}[Parabolic quantum double Schubert polynomials] \label{def:parabolic}
	Following Lam and Shimozono \cite{lam2014quantum}, we define parabolic quantum double Schubert polynomials as follows. Fix an integer $n>0$. A parabolic subgroup $W_P$ of $S_{n}$, which has Coxeter generators $s_1,\ldots,s_{n-1}$, is generated by a subset $P\subseteq \{s_1,\ldots,s_{n-1}\}$. In terms of the subgroup's properties, it is most natural to identify $P$ by the reflections that it \emph{excludes}. Suppose $P$ is generated by $S\setminus \{s_{i_1},\ldots,s_{i_k}\}$ with $1\leq i_1<\cdots<i_k\leq n-1$, and set $i_0=0$ and $i_{k+1}=n$. We define the \emph{block sizes} $n_1,\ldots,n_{k+1}>0$, with
	$$n_1+\cdots+n_{k+1}=n,$$
	by $n_p = i_{p} - i_{p-1}$ for $1\leq p\leq k+1$, and put
	$$N_j = n_1+\cdots+n_j=i_j\qquad(0\leq j\leq k+1),$$
	so that $N_0=0$ and $N_{k+1}=n$. Thus $W_P=S_{n_1}\times\cdots\times S_{n_{k+1}}$ and $SL_n/P$ is the variety of partial flags with subspaces of dimensions $N_1<\cdots<N_k$. The extreme cases are $k=0$, where $W_P=S_n$, $W^P=\{1\}$, $SL_n/P$ is a point and the only polynomial defined below is $\qsch^P_1=1$, and $P=\emptyset$ ($k=n-1$, all $n_p=1$), which recovers the quantum double Schubert polynomials of the complete flag variety.
	
	For such a setup, let $D^P$ be the $n\times n$ matrix with $x_1,\ldots,x_n$ on the diagonal, $-1$ on the superdiagonal, the entry $(-1)^{n_{j+1}+1}q_j$ in position $(N_{j+1},N_{j-1}+1)$ for $1\leq j\leq k$, and all other entries $0$, and let $D^P_j$ be its upper left $N_j\times N_j$ submatrix. Notice that there are precisely $k$ $q$ variables, $q_j$ corresponding to the excluded reflection $s_{i_j}$, and we set
	$$\deg q_j = n_j + n_{j+1}.$$  
	
	Define 
	$$\Delta^P_j(t;x)=\det(D^P_j+t\,\mathrm{Id})\qquad(1\leq j\leq k),$$
	so that for $P=\emptyset$ this is the polynomial $\Delta_j(t\mid x_1,\ldots,x_j)$ above (the matrices differ by a transposition). Then we define the parabolic quantum double Schubert polynomials $\qsch_w^P(x;y)$ for $w\in W^P$ by
	$$\qsch^P_w(x;y)=(-1)^{\ell(w(w_0^P)^{-1})}\partial_{w(w_0^P)^{-1}}^{(y)}\prod_{j=1}^k\ \prod_{i=n-N_{j+1}+1}^{n-N_j} \Delta^P_j(-y_i;x),$$
	where $w_0^P$ is the longest element of $W^P$. For $P=\emptyset$ the product is $\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\Delta_j(-y_{n-j}\mid x_1,\ldots,x_j)=\qsch_{w_0}(x;y)$.
	
	Taken as defined, when we vary $n$ these polynomials are not stable under inclusion and do not form a basis of $\mathbb{Z}[q][x_1,\ldots,x_n;y]$ as a module over $\mathbb{Z}[q][y]$. To obtain stability and a basis, instead we fix $n$ and choose $N>n$ as large as we would like, maintaining $N_{k+1}=n$ and setting $n_{k+2} = N-n$. From a cohomological perspective, this introduces ``extraneous'' polynomials that are indexed by elements not in $W^P$. The full indexing set is $W^{\pcomp{P}}\subset S_\infty$, where
	$$\pcomp{P} = P \cup \{s_{n+1},s_{n+2},\ldots\}$$
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
As equivariant cohomology rings of finite dimensional projective varieties over $\mathbb{C}$ are finitely generated modules over the coefficient ring, it is fully expected that products of double Schubert polynomials may include terms indexed by permutations that do not lie within the indexing set of the basis of Schubert classes. For example, consider the following non-quantum double Schubert polynomial product, with $P=\{s_1,s_3\}$ and basis elements in $S_4$:
\begin{align*}
\sch_{2413}(x;y)\sch_{3412}(x;y)
&= - (\alpha_1+\alpha_2)(\alpha_1+\alpha_2+\alpha_3)(\alpha_2+\alpha_3)\,\sch_{3412}(x;y) \\
&\quad + (\alpha_1+\alpha_2)(\alpha_1+2\alpha_2+2\alpha_3+\alpha_4)\,\sch_{35124}(x;y) \\
&\quad - (\alpha_1+\alpha_2)\,\sch_{361245}(x;y) \\
&\quad - (\alpha_1+2\alpha_2+2\alpha_3+\alpha_4)\,\sch_{45123}(x;y) \\
&\quad + \sch_{461235}(x;y).
\end{align*}
Not only does the product $\qsch_{2413}^{P}(x;y)\qsch_{3412}^{P}(x;y)$ include extra terms indexed by permutations in $W^P$, there are also terms with nonzero coefficients that are in $W^{\pcomp{P}}\setminus W^P$:
\begin{align*}
q_1(\alpha_1\alpha_2+\alpha_1\alpha_3+\alpha_2\alpha_3+\alpha_2^2)\,\qsch_{132}^{P}(x;y)
&+ q_1(\alpha_1+\alpha_2)\,\qsch_{231}^{P}(x;y) \\
&+ q_1(\alpha_2+\alpha_3)\,\qsch_{1423}^{P}(x;y) \\
&+ \color{red}{q_1(\alpha_1+\alpha_2)\,\qsch_{13254}^{\pcomp{P}}(x;y)} \\
&+ \color{red}{q_1\,\qsch_{14253}^{\pcomp{P}}(x;y)} \\
&+ q_1\,\qsch_{15234}^{P}(x;y) \\
&+ q_1\,\qsch_{2413}^{P}(x;y)
\end{align*}
\end{example}


\begin{definition}[Coroot lattices, $\langle d\rangle_i$, Peterson--Woodward lifts] \label{def:pwlift}
	Let $\alpha_1,\alpha_2,\ldots$ be the simple roots of $S_\infty$ and $\alpha_i^\vee$ the simple coroots, and let
	$$Q^\vee=\bigoplus_{i\geq1}\mathbb Z\alpha_i^\vee$$
	be the coroot lattice. We write its elements as integer vectors $d=(d_1,d_2,\ldots)$ with finitely many nonzero entries, $d=\sum_id_i\alpha_i^\vee$; the degrees of Section \ref{section:definitions} are the \emph{effective} elements, those with all $d_i\geq0$, and $q^d=\prod_iq_i^{d_i}$. (When working in $S_n$ we take $i\leq n-1$ and $d\in\mathbb Z^{n-1}$.) For $d\in Q^\vee$ and $i\geq1$ put
	$$\langle d\rangle_i = \langle\alpha_i,d\rangle=2d_i - d_{i-1} - d_{i + 1},$$
	with the convention $d_0=0$ (and $d_i=0$ for $i\geq n$ when working in $S_n$), and for $i<j$
	$$\langle d\rangle_{ij} = \sum_{l=i}^{j-1}\langle d\rangle_l=\langle\alpha_i+\cdots+\alpha_{j-1},d\rangle=\langle e_i-e_j,d\rangle.$$
	We write $\delta_i\in Q^\vee$ for the simple coroot $\alpha_i^\vee$, the vector with a single $1$ in position $i$, and $|d|=\sum_id_i$.

	For a parabolic subgroup $W_P$ generated by $P$, let
	$$Q^\vee_P=\bigoplus_{s_i\in P}\mathbb Z\alpha_i^\vee=\{d\in Q^\vee\mid d_i=0\text{ whenever }s_i\notin P\}$$
	be the coroot lattice of the Levi subgroup of $P$. The degrees of $QH_T^*(G/P)$ are indexed by the quotient $Q^\vee/Q^\vee_P$, which we identify with $\bigoplus_{s_i\notin P}\mathbb Z\alpha_i^\vee$ by sending the class of $d$ to $d_P:=(d_i)_{s_i\notin P}$, the vector of coordinates of $d$ at the excluded reflections; $d_P$ is called effective if these coordinates are nonnegative. In the notation of Definition \ref{def:parabolic}, the coordinate of $d_P$ at the excluded reflection $s_{i_j}$ is the exponent of $q_j$, so that $q^{d_P}=\prod_{j=1}^kq_j^{d_{i_j}}$. (For $P=\emptyset$, $Q^\vee_P=0$ and $d_P=d$.)

	Let $d_P\in Q^\vee/Q^\vee_P$. A \emph{Peterson--Woodward lift} of $d_P$ is a $d\in Q^\vee$ such that $d+ Q_P^\vee=d_P$ and $\langle d\rangle_{ij}\in \{0,-1\}$ for all $i<j$ such that $s_{i},\ldots,s_{j-1}\in P$. In the language of roots, the second condition says that $\langle\alpha,d\rangle\in\{0,-1\}$ for every positive root $\alpha$ of the Levi subgroup of $P$, these being the roots $\alpha_i+\cdots+\alpha_{j-1}$ with $s_i,\ldots,s_{j-1}\in P$. Every $d_P$ has exactly one Peterson--Woodward lift \cite{woodward2005d}; we denote it by $d$ or $\lambda_B$ when $d_P=\lambda_P$.
 \end{definition}

 \begin{method}[Checking the Peterson-Woodward lift property]
 While backing out a Peterson-Woodward lift is difficult, going in the other direction is not. Suppose $d\in Q^\vee$ and $P$ is parabolic. If there is any $i$ with $s_i\in P$ such that $\langle d\rangle_i\notin\{0,-1\}$, then $d$ is not a Peterson-Woodward lift. If this condition does hold, let $I_1,\ldots,I_m$ be the maximal intervals of consecutive indices $i$ with $s_i\in P$. Since $\langle d\rangle_{ij}=\sum_{l=i}^{j-1}\langle d\rangle_l$, the condition $\langle d\rangle_{ij}\in\{0,-1\}$ for all $i<j$ with $s_i,\ldots,s_{j-1}\in P$ holds if and only if each interval $I_r$ contains at most one index $l$ with $\langle d\rangle_l=-1$. If and only if this is the case, $d$ is the Peterson-Woodward lift of a unique $d_P\in Q^\vee/Q^\vee_P$, namely its image $d+Q^\vee_P$, which we denote by $\pi_P(d)$. In that case we let
$$z_P(d)=\{\,i\mid s_i\in P,\ \langle d\rangle_i=0\,\}$$
and write $w_{z_P(d)}$ for the longest element of the parabolic subgroup generated by $\{s_i\mid i\in z_P(d)\}$, and $w_P$ for the longest element of $W_P$. 
 \end{method}

 \begin{example}
 Let $n=3$ and $P=\{s_1\}$, so that $W_P=S_2\times S_1$, $SL_3/P=\mathbb P^2$, $Q^\vee_P=\mathbb Z\alpha_1^\vee=\{(d_1,0)\}$, and $Q^\vee/Q^\vee_P\cong\mathbb Z$ via $d\mapsto d_2$. The lifts of $d_P=1$ are the vectors $(d_1,1)$, with $\langle d\rangle_1=2d_1-1$; the only one with $\langle d\rangle_1\in\{0,-1\}$ is $d=(0,1)$, with $\langle d\rangle_1=-1$ and $z_P(d)=\emptyset$, so that $w_Pw_{z_P(d)}=s_1$. For $d_P=2$ the lifts are $(d_1,2)$ with $\langle d\rangle_1=2d_1-2$, and the Peterson--Woodward lift is $d=(1,2)$, with $\langle d\rangle_1=0$, $z_P(d)=\{1\}$ and $w_Pw_{z_P(d)}=1$.
 \end{example}

 This algorithm is polynomial in the length of $d$, which in almost all currently feasible cases is quite small. In contrast, a naive search for $d$ with $d_P$ as the input has an exponential search space, though a more refined algorithm may exist.

 The parabolic triple quantum Schubert structure constants $c_{u,v}^{w,d_P}(y;z)$ are defined by
$$\qsch^P_u(x;y)\qsch^P_v(x;z)=\sum_{w\in W^{\pcomp P},\,d_P}q^{d_P}\,c_{u,v}^{w,d_P}(y;z)\,\qsch^P_w(x;y).$$
We prove the following result in Section \ref{section:pw}, as a consequence of the Peterson--Woodward comparison formula of Huang and Li \cite{huang2015equivariant} and Lemma \ref{lemma:quantumtoordinary}.

 \begin{proposition} \label{prop:parabolic}
Let $W_P$ be a parabolic subgroup of $S_\infty$ with $P=\pcomp{P}$ and let $u,v,w\in W^{P}$. Then for any $d_P\in Q^\vee/Q_P^\vee$ with Peterson--Woodward lift $d$, we have that
$$c_{u,v}^{w,d_P}(y;z) = c_{u,v}^{ww_Pw_{z_P(d)},d}(y;z).$$
\end{proposition}

Thus, though we lack a simple functoriality property, we can reduce the computation of parabolic triple quantum Schubert structure constants completely to the complete flag variety case with an efficient algorithm. We obtain a nontrivial corollary from this.

\begin{corollary}[Triple quantum Schubert positivity for the parabolic case] \label{corollary:parabolicpositivity}
Let $W_P$ be a parabolic subgroup with $P=\pcomp P$ and let $u,v,w\in W^P$. Then for every $d_P$ the parabolic structure constant $c_{u,v}^{w,d_P}(y;z)$ exhibits triple Schubert positivity. In particular this holds when $u,v,w$ are $k$-Grassmannian and $P$ is the corresponding maximal parabolic subgroup ($n_1=k$, $n_2=n-k$).
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By Proposition \ref{prop:parabolic}, $c_{u,v}^{w,d_P}(y;z)$ equals a structure constant $c_{u,v}^{w',d}(y;z)$ of the complete flag variety, which lies in $\mathbb Z_{\geq0}[y_i-z_j]$ by Theorem \ref{theorem:positivity}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The Grassmannian case] \label{remark:grassmannian}
When $P$ is a maximal parabolic subgroup, $G/P=\mathrm{Gr}(k,n)$, and at $z=y$ the parabolic constants $c_{u,v}^{w,d_P}(y;y)$ are the structure constants of $QH_T^*(\mathrm{Gr}(k,n))$, whose Graham positivity is Mihalcea's theorem \cite{mihalcea2006positivity}. In this case a positive \emph{combinatorial} rule is available. The equivariant ``quantum equals classical'' theorem of Buch and Mihalcea \cite{buch2011qkgrass} (the equivariant form of \cite{buch2003gwgrass}) identifies each $c_{u,v}^{w,d_P}(y;y)$ with an equivariant structure constant of a two-step flag variety $\mathrm{Fl}(k-d,k+d;n)$, and Buch's mutation algorithm for puzzles \cite{buch2015mutations} expresses the latter as a Graham-positive sum over equivariant puzzles; together they give a Littlewood--Richardson rule for $QH_T^*(\mathrm{Gr}(k,n))$ (see also the earlier equivariant quantum Pieri rule and recursion of Mihalcea \cite{mihalcea2006equivariant}, and the non-equivariant puzzle rule for two-step flag varieties of \cite{buch2016puzzle}). Proposition \ref{prop:parabolic} describes the same numbers as structure constants of the complete flag variety, with the degree $d_P$ replaced by its Peterson--Woodward lift and $w$ by $ww_Pw_{z_P(d)}$, which is how the package \cite{schubmult} computes them. For $z\neq y$ the triple Grassmannian constants are determined by the equivariant ones through the parabolic quantum Cauchy identity (proof of Corollary \ref{corollary:pwz}), $c_{u,v}^{w,d_P}(y;z)=\sum_{v_1\cdot v_2=v}c_{u,v_2}^{w,d_P}(y;y)\,\sch_{v_1}(y;z)$, but this expression is not manifestly positive in the differences $y_i-z_j$, and we do not know a positive combinatorial rule for them.
\end{remark}


\section{The quantum Pieri formula} \label{section:pieri}

\begin{definition}[$\tom{k}$, $\fx_{k}(u,w)$, $q_k(u,w)$, $E_{p,k}(y;z)$, $\wte(y_A;z)$] \label{definition:pieri}
We define a relation $\tom{k}$ on pairs of permutations, introduced in its non-quantum form by Sottile \cite{sottile}, which was used originally to indicate when the coefficient of a Schubert polynomial is nonzero in a suitable application of the Pieri formula. In the generalization to double Schubert polynomials in \cite{samuelmolev}, the same relation applies to all nonzero coefficients in the expansion of the generalized Pieri formula. The relation plays the same role in our case. 

Given $u,w\in S_\infty$ and a positive integer $k$, we declare that $u\tom{k} w$ if there exists a $p$ with $0\leq p\leq k$ such that there are transpositions $t_{a_1b_1},\ldots,t_{a_pb_p}$ satisfying 
\begin{enumerate}
\item $a_i\leq k<b_i$ for all $i$
\item $a_i\neq a_j$ whenever $i\neq j$
\item $b_i\geq b_j$ whenever $i<j$
\item We have either
$$\ell(ut_{a_1b_1}\cdots t_{a_ib_i})=\ell(ut_{a_1b_1}\cdots t_{a_{i-1}b_{i-1}})+1$$ 
or 
$$\ell(ut_{a_1b_1}\cdots t_{a_ib_i})=\ell(ut_{a_1b_1}\cdots t_{a_{i-1}b_{i-1}})-2(b_i-a_i)+1$$
for all $i$
\item $w=ut_{a_1b_1}\cdots t_{a_pb_p}$
\end{enumerate}
In contrast to the non-quantum case, $u\tom{k} w$ does not imply that $\ell(u) \leq \ell(w)$, as the final condition can cause the length of the permutation to drop. For intuition, the condition $\ell(ut_{ab}) = \ell(u) - 2(b - a) + 1$ means that if we write the reflection $t_{ab}$ as
$$s_{b}s_{b-1}\cdots s_{a+1}s_as_{a+1}\cdots s_{b-1}s_b$$
then each simple reflection in this word cancels a corresponding simple reflection in a reduced word for $u$, as opposite to the length-increase case where there is a word for which precisely one simple reflection is inserted, regardless of the length of the reflection as a permutation.

If $u\tom{k} w$, define 
$$\fx_{k}(u,w)=\{u(i)\mid i\leq k\mbox{ and }u(i)=w(i)\}$$
and
$$n_k(u,w)=k-\lvert\fx_k(u,w)\rvert.$$
Also, we define $q_k(u,w)$ recursively. If $u=w$, define $q_k(u,w)=1$. If we have defined $q_k(u,w')$ with $w'=ut_{a_1b_1}\cdots t_{a_{p-1}b_{p-1}}$, suppose $\ell(w't_{a_pb_p})=\ell(w')+1$. Then define
$$q_k(u,w)=q_k(u,w')$$
If instead $\ell(w't_{a_pb_p})=\ell(w')-2(b_p-a_p)+1$, define
$$q_k(u,w)=q_k(u,w')q_{a_pb_p}$$

Suppose $y$ and $z$ are sets of variables and $0\leq p\leq k$ are integers. We define $E_{p,k}(y;z)$ to be the factorial elementary symmetric polynomial in $y$ and $z$. For specificity, this is the double Schubert polynomial $\sch_{c[p,k]}(y;z)$ where
$$c[p,k] = s_{k-p+1}\cdots s_{k-1}s_k$$
If $p=0$, the polynomial is identically $1$. If $p=k$, $E_{k,k}(y;z)$ can be simply written as
$$E_{k,k}(y;z)=\prod_{i=1}^k(y_i-z_1)$$
An explicit formula can be written for general $p$ without too much trouble as well, using e.g. \cite{molev1999littlewood}:
$$E_{p,k}(y;z)=\sum_{1\leq i_1<\cdots<i_p\leq k}\prod_{j=1}^p(y_{i_j}-z_{i_j-j+1})$$

For a set $A=\{i_1,\ldots,i_{|A|}\}$, we define
$$\wte(y_A;z)=\prod_{i\in A}(y_i-z_1)=E_{|A|,|A|}(y_{i_1},\ldots,y_{i_{|A|}};z),$$
the specialization of the factorial elementary polynomial to which the coefficients of the Pieri formula reduce. It will be used to express those coefficients.
\end{definition}


\begin{definition}[Quantum factorial elementary polynomials $\qelem_{p,k}(x;z)$] \label{def:qelem}
For $0\leq p\leq k$ we define
$$\qelem_{p,k}(x;z)=\qsch_{c[p,k]}(x;z),$$
the quantum double Schubert polynomial of the Grassmannian permutation $c[p,k]=s_{k-p+1}\cdots s_k$, whose diagram is a single column of height $p$ with descent at $k$. At $q=0$ this is $E_{p,k}(x;z)$. We also set $\qelem_{0,k}=1$ and $\qelem_{p,k}=0$ for $p<0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma} \label{lemma:qelemkk}
For $k\geq1$,
$$\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)=\Delta_k(-z_1\mid x_1,\ldots,x_k)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}(-z_1)^{k-j}e_j^{(q)}(x_1,\ldots,x_k).$$
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The permutation $c[k,k]=s_1s_2\cdots s_k$ has a unique reduced word, so its reduced factorizations are $v_1=s_1\cdots s_j$, $v_2=s_{j+1}\cdots s_k$ for $0\leq j\leq k$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:quantumtoordinary} with $y=0$, $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)=\sum_{j}\sch_{s_1\cdots s_j}(0;z)\,\qsch_{s_{j+1}\cdots s_k}(x)$. Here $s_1\cdots s_j$ is dominant with code $(1^j)$, so $\sch_{s_1\cdots s_j}(x;z)=\prod_{i\leq j}(x_i-z_1)$ and $\sch_{s_1\cdots s_j}(0;z)=(-z_1)^j$; and $s_{j+1}\cdots s_k$ is the Grassmannian permutation with descent at $k$ and a single column of height $k-j$, whose Schubert polynomial $e_{k-j}(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$ is a standard elementary monomial, so that its quantum Schubert polynomial is $e^{(q)}_{k-j}(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$ \cite{fomin1997quantum}. Summing gives the second expression, which equals $\Delta_k(-z_1\mid x_1,\ldots,x_k)$ by the definition of the quantum elementary polynomials.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The Pieri formula] \label{theorem:pieri}
Suppose $u\in S_\infty$ and $k\geq 1$. Then
$$\qsch_u(x;y)\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)=\sum_{u\tom{k} w}q_k(u,w)\wte(y_{\fx_{k}(u,w)};z)\qsch_w(x;y).$$
\end{theorem}

The proof is given in Section \ref{section:proofpieri}. Note that the right-hand side is manifestly triple Schubert positive, in accordance with Theorem \ref{theorem:positivity}, and that at $q=0$ it reduces to the case $p=k$ of the Pieri formula of \cite[Theorem~7.1]{samuelmolev}. The general-degree version, Theorem \ref{theorem:pierigeneral}, replaces $\wte(y_{\fx_k(u,w)};z)$ by $E_{p-n_k(u,w),\,k-n_k(u,w)}(y_{\fx_k(u,w)};z)$.

Triple Schubert positivity does not by itself imply Graham positivity at $z=y$: a difference $y_i-y_j$ with $i<j$ is not a nonnegative combination of the negative simple roots $y_{l+1}-y_l$. The Pieri coefficients nevertheless are Graham-positive after specialization, for the same reason as in \cite[Section~7]{samuelmolev}: they are factorial elementary polynomials evaluated at an increasing sequence of $y$-variables.

\begin{corollary}[Graham positivity of the Pieri coefficients] \label{corollary:pierigraham}
Let $u\tom{k}w$, $n=n_k(u,w)$ and $0\leq p\leq k$. The specialization $E_{p-n,\,k-n}(y_{\fx_k(u,w)};z)\big|_{z=y}$ is a polynomial in the differences $y_{l+1}-y_l$ with nonnegative integer coefficients. Consequently, at $z=y$, every coefficient in Theorems \ref{theorem:pieri} and \ref{theorem:pierigeneral} lies in $\mathbb Z_{\geq0}[y_{l+1}-y_l][q_1,q_2,\ldots]$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Write $\fx_k(u,w)=\{a_1<a_2<\cdots<a_{k-n}\}$. Since the $a_i$ are distinct positive integers, $a_i\geq i$ for all $i$. By the formula of Definition \ref{definition:pieri},
$$E_{p-n,\,k-n}(y_{\fx_k(u,w)};z)=\sum_{1\leq i_1<\cdots<i_{p-n}\leq k-n}\ \prod_{j=1}^{p-n}\big(y_{a_{i_j}}-z_{i_j-j+1}\big),$$
and at $z=y$ each factor becomes $y_{a_{i_j}}-y_{i_j-j+1}$ with $a_{i_j}\geq i_j\geq i_j-j+1$, which is the sum $\sum_{l=i_j-j+1}^{a_{i_j}-1}(y_{l+1}-y_l)$ of negative simple roots (empty, hence $0$, when $a_{i_j}=i_j-j+1$). The product and the sum have nonnegative integer coefficients, and $q_k(u,w)$ is a monomial.
\end{proof}



\newcommand{\lom}{\widetilde{\lambda}}
\newcommand{\mom}{\widetilde{\mu}}
\newcommand{\thop}{\widetilde{\theta}}
\newcommand{\medperm}{\overline{\mu}}
\newcommand{\medtheta}{\overline{\theta}}
\newcommand{\Tom}[1]{\xRightarrow{#1}}
\newcommand{\dom}{\mu}

\section{Expansion formulas and the multiplication algorithms} \label{section:algorithms}

\subsection{Dominant shapes}

\begin{definition}[$\code$, (strict) dominant permutations, $\theta$, $\dom$, $\thop$, $\mom$, medium theta $\medtheta$, $\medperm$] \label{def:codetheta}
We say that a permutation $\mu$ is \emph{dominant} if $\code_i(\mu)\geq \code_{i+1}(\mu)$ for all $i$. A permutation $\mu$ is said to be \emph{strict dominant} if $\mu$ is dominant and whenever $\code_i(\mu)\neq 0$ we have $\code_i(\mu)>\code_{i+1}(\mu)$. For a permutation $v\in S_\infty$, we define a dominant permutation $\dom(v)$ recursively as follows. If $v$ is already dominant, define $\mu(v)=v$. Otherwise, let $i$ be the maximal index such that $\code_i(v)<\code_{i+1}(v)$, and define
$$\dom(v)=\dom(vs_i)$$
Define $\theta_i(v)$ to be $\code_i(\dom(v))$.

Similarly, for a permutation $v\in S_\infty$, we define a strict dominant permutation $\mom(v)$ recursively as follows. If $v$ is already strict dominant, define $\mom(v)=v$. Otherwise, let $i$ be the maximal index such that $\code_{i+1}(v)\neq 0$ and $\code_i(v)\leq \code_{i+1}(v)$, then define
$$\mom(v)=\mom(vs_i)$$
Define $\thop_i(v)$ to be $\code_i(\mom(v))$.

Between these two lies the \emph{medium theta} $\medtheta(v)$, the shape actually iterated by the quantum multiplication algorithm (the routine \texttt{medium\_theta} of \cite{schubmult}). Call an index $i\geq1$ \emph{defective} for $v$ if either
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $\code_i(v)<\code_{i+1}(v)$, or
\item[(b)] $\code_i(v)=\code_{i+1}(v)\neq0$, $i>1$, and $\code_{i-1}(v)\leq\code_i(v)+1$.
\end{enumerate}
If $v$ has no defective index, define $\medperm(v)=v$; otherwise let $i$ be the \emph{minimal} defective index and define
$$\medperm(v)=\medperm(vs_i).$$
Define $\medtheta_i(v)=\code_i(\medperm(v))$ and $|\medtheta(v)|=\sum_i\medtheta_i(v)$. In both cases (a) and (b) one has $\code_i(v)\leq\code_{i+1}(v)$, which forces $v(i)<v(i+1)$; hence $\ell(vs_i)=\ell(v)+1$, $\code(vs_i)$ is obtained from $\code(v)$ by replacing $(\code_i,\code_{i+1})$ with $(\code_{i+1}+1,\code_i)$ (in case (b): by incrementing $\code_i$), and the recursion terminates, since a defective index has $\code_{i+1}(v)\neq0$, so that $v\in S_n$ implies $vs_i\in S_n$, and the length increases at each step. By construction $\medtheta(v)$ is weakly decreasing, so $\medperm(v)$ is dominant; a pair of equal consecutive nonzero parts $\medtheta_i(v)=\medtheta_{i+1}(v)$ occurs only when $i=1$ or $\medtheta_{i-1}(v)\geq\medtheta_i(v)+2$, in particular no three consecutive nonzero parts are equal; and $\medperm(v)=v\cdot y$ is a reduced factorization with $\ell(\medperm(v))=|\medtheta(v)|$. Thus $\medtheta(v)$ permits some, but not all, repeated parts, and lies between $\theta(v)$ (arbitrary repeats) and $\thop(v)$ (no repeats). The number of nonzero parts of $\medtheta(v)$ equals the number of nonzero parts of $\code(v)$, since neither (a) nor (b) creates or destroys a zero. We say that a dominant permutation $\mu$ has \emph{medium shape} if its code satisfies the condition just stated: whenever two consecutive nonzero parts $\code_i(\mu)=\code_{i+1}(\mu)$ are equal, either $i=1$ or $\code_{i-1}(\mu)\geq\code_i(\mu)+2$.
\end{definition}

The three shapes are related by the right weak order, in which $u\leq_Rw$ means $w=u\cdot y$ reduced, equivalently $N(u)\subseteq N(w)$ for the sets of \emph{value inversions} $N(u)=\{(a,b):a<b,\ u^{-1}(b)<u^{-1}(a)\}$ \cite[Proposition~3.1.3]{combcox}. Recall that a permutation is dominant if and only if it avoids the pattern $132$; it is strict dominant if and only if it is decreasing and then increasing, i.e.\ if and only if it avoids both $132$ and $231$ (a strict dominant permutation has $\mu(i)>\mu(i+1)$ for all $i$ up to its last descent, and conversely a permutation without peaks has no $132$ and no $231$).

\begin{lemma} \label{lemma:forcedmove}
Let $v\in S_\infty$ and let $i\geq1$ be such that $v(i)<v(i+1)$ and $\code_{i+1}(v)\neq0$. Then every $w\geq_Rv$ avoiding $132$ and $231$ satisfies $w\geq_Rvs_i$. If moreover $\code_i(v)<\code_{i+1}(v)$, then every $w\geq_Rv$ avoiding $132$ satisfies $w\geq_Rvs_i$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put $a=v(i)<b=v(i+1)$; then $N(vs_i)=N(v)\cup\{(a,b)\}$, so we must show that $b$ precedes $a$ in $w$. Since $\code_{i+1}(v)\neq0$ there is a value $c<b$ at a position $j>i+1$ of $v$, and $(c,b)\in N(v)\subseteq N(w)$ says that $b$ precedes $c$ in $w$. Suppose that $a$ precedes $b$ in $w$. If $c<a$, then $(c,a)\in N(v)\subseteq N(w)$, so $w$ contains $a,b,c$ in this order, a $231$ pattern. If $a<c<b$, then $w$ contains $a,b,c$ in this order, a $132$ pattern. In the first assertion both cases are excluded. For the second, note that $\code_{i+1}(v)-\code_i(v)=\#\{j>i+1:a<v(j)<b\}$ when $v(i)<v(i+1)$, so $\code_i(v)<\code_{i+1}(v)$ allows us to choose $c$ with $a<c<b$, and only the $132$ case arises.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Weak order characterization] \label{prop:weakorder}
Let $v\in S_\infty$ and let $\mathcal P=\mathcal P(v)$ be the set of indices $i$ with $\medtheta_i(v)=\medtheta_{i+1}(v)\neq0$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $\dom(v)$ is the minimum, for $\leq_R$, of the dominant permutations $w\geq_Rv$. (This is the upper projection of $v$ to the Tamari lattice, realized as the set of $132$-avoiding permutations in the weak order \cite{bjorner1997shellable}, i.e.\ to the quotient of the weak order by the sylvester congruence \cite{reading2004congruences,reading2006cambrian}.)
\item[(ii)] $\mom(v)$ is the minimum of the strict dominant permutations $w\geq_Rv$.
\item[(iii)] The simple reflections $s_i$, $i\in\mathcal P$, commute pairwise, and
$$\mom(v)=\medperm(v)\cdot\prod_{i\in\mathcal P}s_i$$
is a reduced factorization. Equivalently, $\thop(v)$ is obtained from $\medtheta(v)$ by increasing by one the first entry of each retained equal pair, and $|\thop(v)|=|\medtheta(v)|+|\mathcal P(v)|$.
\end{enumerate}
In (i) and (ii) the recursions of Definition \ref{def:codetheta} may take the defective indices in any order; $\medperm(v)$, by contrast, depends on the order (for $v=2341$ the permutations $4231=\medperm(v)$ and $3421$ are both minimal among the medium shape permutations above $v$), and it is normalized by the choice of the minimal defective index.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
(i) Each step $v\mapsto vs_i$ of the recursion for $\dom$ has $\code_i(v)<\code_{i+1}(v)$, so by Lemma \ref{lemma:forcedmove} every dominant $w\geq_Rv$ satisfies $w\geq_Rvs_i$; by induction on $\ell(\dom(v))-\ell(v)$, $w\geq_R\dom(v)$, and $\dom(v)$ is dominant and $\geq_Rv$. (ii) is identical, each step of the recursion for $\mom$ having $\code_i(v)\leq\code_{i+1}(v)$, i.e.\ $v(i)<v(i+1)$, and $\code_{i+1}(v)\neq0$, so that the first assertion of Lemma \ref{lemma:forcedmove} applies. (iii) Put $\mu=\medperm(v)$ and $w=\mom(v)$. Each step of the recursion for $\medperm$ is of type (a) or (b) of Definition \ref{def:codetheta}, and in both cases $v(i)<v(i+1)$ and $\code_{i+1}(v)\neq0$; so Lemma \ref{lemma:forcedmove} and induction give $w\geq_R\mu$. The indices in $\mathcal P$ are pairwise at distance $\geq2$, since no three consecutive parts of $\medtheta(v)$ are equal, so the $s_i$ commute. For $i\in\mathcal P$ we have $\mu(i)<\mu(i+1)$ (as $\code_i(\mu)=\code_{i+1}(\mu)$) and $\code_{i+1}(\mu)\neq0$; applying Lemma \ref{lemma:forcedmove} successively to $\mu,\ \mu s_{i_1},\ \mu s_{i_1}s_{i_2},\ldots$ (the positions $i,i+1$ and the entry $\code_{i+1}$ being unaffected by the previous $s_{i'}$, $|i-i'|\geq2$) gives $w\geq_R\mu':=\mu\prod_{i\in\mathcal P}s_i$. Each $s_i$ increases the length, replacing $(\code_i,\code_{i+1})=(k,k)$ by $(k+1,k)$, so $\mu'=\mu\cdot\prod s_i$ is reduced and $\code(\mu')$ is strictly decreasing on its nonzero part (the part preceding a retained pair is $\geq k+2>k+1$): $\mu'$ is strict dominant and $\mu'\geq_R\mu\geq_Rv$, whence $w\leq_R\mu'$ by (ii). Thus $w=\mu'$. For the final remark, $N(2341)=\{(1,2),(1,3),(1,4)\}$ is contained in $N(4231)$ and in $N(3421)$, both of which are medium shape with codes $(3,1,1)$ and $(2,2,1)$, and neither is below the other.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Expansion of a quantum double Schubert polynomial}

\begin{definition}[$\xRightarrow{i}$, $\ell(u,v)$, $\sigma_i(u,v)$, $D_i(u,v)$, $z_D$] \label{def:itom}
Let $u,v\in S_\infty$ and $i\geq 1$ be an integer. We define $u\Tom{i} v$ if there exist distinct integers $b_1,b_2,\ldots,b_k$ such that $b_j\neq i$ for all $j$, $\ell(ut_{i,b_1}\cdots t_{i,b_j})=\ell(u)+j$ for all $j$, and 
$$v=ut_{i,b_1}\cdots t_{i,b_k}.$$
Thus $u\Tom{i}v$ means that $v$ is reached from $u$ by a chain of Bruhat covers $u\lessdot ut_{ib_1}\lessdot ut_{ib_1}t_{ib_2}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot v$, each of which exchanges the position $i$ with a position not used before. The sequence $b_1,\ldots,b_k$ is determined by $u$ and $v$: the product $u^{-1}v=t_{ib_1}\cdots t_{ib_k}$ is the cycle $i\mapsto b_k\mapsto b_{k-1}\mapsto\cdots\mapsto b_1\mapsto i$, from which the $b_j$ are read off. We write $\ell(u,v)=\ell(v)-\ell(u)=k$ and define
$$\sigma_i(u,v)=\#\{j\mid b_j<i\},\qquad D_i(u,v)=\{u(i)\}\cup\{u(j)\mid u(j)\neq v(j)\}=\{u(i),u(b_1),\ldots,u(b_k)\},$$
a set of $k+1$ values. Finally, for a finite set $D=\{d_1<\cdots<d_m\}$ of positive integers with $m=k-p+1$ we write $E_{p,k}(x;z_D)=E_{p,k}(x;z_{d_1},\ldots,z_{d_m})$ and $\qelem_{p,k}(x;z_D)=\qelem_{p,k}(x;z_{d_1},\ldots,z_{d_m})$; since these polynomials are symmetric in $z_1,\ldots,z_{k-p+1}$ (Lemma \ref{lemma:Ehe}), the order in which the elements of $D$ are listed is immaterial. As in Definition \ref{def:qelem}, $E_{p,k}=\qelem_{p,k}=0$ when $p<0$.
\end{definition}

The following formulas express a (quantum) double Schubert polynomial as a signed sum of products of (quantum) factorial elementary polynomials in nested initial alphabets; they are the $v$-side input of the multiplication algorithm, and they are proved in Section \ref{section:proofvside}. All of them start from a dominant permutation $\mu$ lying above $v^{-1}$ in the right weak order, that is $\mu=v^{-1}\cdot y$ with $\ell(\mu)=\ell(v)+\ell(y)$; the permutations $\dom(v^{-1})$, $\mom(v^{-1})$ and $\medperm(v^{-1})$ are of this kind, since each step of their construction multiplies on the right by a simple reflection $s_i$ with $\code_i\leq\code_{i+1}$, which forces $v(i)<v(i+1)$ and so increases the length. The implementation uses $\mu=\dom(v^{-1})$ in the classical case, and the parts of $\code(\mu)$ are then the $\theta_i(v^{-1})$ of Definition \ref{def:codetheta}. In the quantum case the dominant permutation cannot be arbitrary, because the quantum polynomial $\qsch_{\mu^{-1}}(x;z)$ which is being expanded factors into a product of quantum factorial elementary polynomials only when the column heights of the diagram of $\mu^{-1}$, which are the parts of $\code(\mu)$, are pairwise distinct (Lemma \ref{lemma:dominantfactor}); for repeated column heights it is \emph{not} a product (see Section \ref{section:additional}). Theorem \ref{theorem:quandouble} accordingly uses the strict shape $\mom(v^{-1})$. The implementation (the routine \texttt{schubmult\_q\_double\_fast} of \cite{schubmult}) uses the smaller intermediate shape $\medperm(v^{-1})$, in which an equal pair of columns is allowed when there is room for it; the quantization of such a pair is a single ``doubled'' factor, which Theorem \ref{theorem:quandoublemed} isolates and which the algorithm multiplies out by the doubled Pieri formula of Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} and Corollary \ref{corollary:lowerdegrees}.

\begin{theorem}[Formula for ordinary double Schubert polynomials] \label{theorem:orddouble}
Let $v\in S_\infty$ and let $\mu$ be a dominant permutation with $\mu=v^{-1}\cdot y$ reduced, for instance $\mu=\dom(v^{-1})$. Let $\theta=\code(\mu)$, with nonzero parts $\theta_1\geq\cdots\geq\theta_k>0$. Then
$$\sch_v(x;z)=\sum_{(v_0,\ldots,v_k)}\prod_{i=1}^k (-1)^{\sigma_i(v_{i-1},v_i)}E_{\theta_i-\ell(v_{i-1},v_i),\,\theta_i}\big(x;z_{D_i(v_{i-1},v_i)}\big),$$
the sum being over all sequences $1=v_0\Tom{1}v_1\Tom{2}v_2\Tom{3}\cdots\Tom{k}v_k=v\mu$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Formula for quantum double Schubert polynomials] \label{theorem:quandouble}
Let $v\in S_\infty$ and let $\mu$ be a strict dominant permutation with $\mu=v^{-1}\cdot y$ reduced, for instance $\mu=\mom(v^{-1})$. Let $\thop=\code(\mu)$, with nonzero parts $\thop_1>\cdots>\thop_k>0$. Then
$$\qsch_v(x;z)=\sum_{(v_0,\ldots,v_k)}\prod_{i=1}^k (-1)^{\sigma_i(v_{i-1},v_i)}\qelem_{\thop_i-\ell(v_{i-1},v_i),\,\thop_i}\big(x;z_{D_i(v_{i-1},v_i)}\big),$$
the sum being over all sequences $1=v_0\Tom{1}v_1\Tom{2}\cdots\Tom{k}v_k=v\mu$.
\end{theorem}

For the medium shape we need a name for the quantization of a product of two factorial elementary polynomials in the same $x$-variables. Let $\mathcal Q$ be the quantization map of Fomin, Gelfand and Postnikov (Lemma \ref{lemma:quantumtoordinary}), extended linearly over all coefficient variables, and for $0\leq p_1,p_2\leq k$ and two alphabets $z,z'$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qelemdouble}
\qelem_{p_1,p_2;k}(x;z,z'):=\mathcal Q\big(E_{p_1,k}(x;z)\,E_{p_2,k}(x;z')\big),
\end{equation}
the \emph{doubled quantum factorial elementary polynomial}; for $p_1=p_2=k$ it is the quantum double Schubert polynomial $\qsch_{d_2[k]}$ of the $k\times2$ rectangle in the variables $z_1,z'_1$ (Section \ref{section:additional}). It is \emph{not} the product $\qelem_{p_1,k}(x;z)\qelem_{p_2,k}(x;z')$ in general.

\begin{theorem}[Formula for quantum double Schubert polynomials, medium shape] \label{theorem:quandoublemed}
Let $v\in S_\infty$ and let $\mu$ be a dominant permutation of medium shape (Definition \ref{def:codetheta}) with $\mu=v^{-1}\cdot y$ reduced, for instance $\mu=\medperm(v^{-1})$. Let $\medtheta=\code(\mu)$, with nonzero parts $\medtheta_1\geq\cdots\geq\medtheta_m>0$, let $\mathcal P=\{i:\medtheta_i=\medtheta_{i+1}\}$ be the set of \emph{paired} indices and $\mathcal S=\{1,\ldots,m\}\setminus(\mathcal P\cup(\mathcal P+1))$ the set of \emph{single} indices. Then
$$\qsch_v(x;z)=\sum_{(v_0,\ldots,v_m)}\ \prod_{i=1}^m(-1)^{\sigma_i(v_{i-1},v_i)}\ \prod_{i\in\mathcal S}\qelem_{\medtheta_i-\ell_i,\,\medtheta_i}\big(x;z_{D_i}\big)\ \prod_{i\in\mathcal P}\qelem_{\medtheta_i-\ell_i,\,\medtheta_i-\ell_{i+1};\,\medtheta_i}\big(x;z_{D_i},z_{D_{i+1}}\big),$$
where $\ell_i=\ell(v_{i-1},v_i)$, $D_i=D_i(v_{i-1},v_i)$, and the sum is over all sequences $1=v_0\Tom{1}v_1\Tom{2}\cdots\Tom{m}v_m=v\mu$. For a strict dominant $\mu$ this is Theorem \ref{theorem:quandouble}.
\end{theorem}

By Proposition \ref{prop:weakorder}(iii), $|\thop(v^{-1})|=|\medtheta(v^{-1})|+|\mathcal P|$: the medium shape is smaller than the strict shape by exactly the number of retained pairs (for instance $\code(v^{-1})=(1,1)$ gives $\medtheta=(1,1)$, $\thop=(2,1)$, and $\code(v^{-1})=(2,2,2)$ gives $\medtheta=(4,2,2)$, $\thop=(4,3,2)$). Since $\sum_i\ell_i=|\code(\mu)|-\ell(v)$ for every chain in these formulas, a smaller shape means shorter chains in the $v$-side sum and, in the multiplication algorithm, $u$-side Pieri steps of smaller degree; this is the reason the implementation prefers $\medperm(v^{-1})$ to $\mom(v^{-1})$. Proposition \ref{prop:weakorder}(iii) also explains how Theorem \ref{theorem:quandoublemed} arises from Theorem \ref{theorem:quandouble}: the dominant polynomial of the medium shape is obtained from that of the strict shape by one divided difference per retained pair, which turns the product of the two adjacent factors into the doubled factor (Corollary \ref{corollary:mediumfactor}).

\subsection{The classical algorithm}

Coupled with the Pieri formulas, these formulas give an algorithm for computing products of (quantum) double Schubert polynomials, which is the one implemented in \cite{schubmult}. (Algorithms for computing quantum Schubert polynomials and Gromov--Witten invariants of partial flag manifolds were given by Buch \cite{buch2005partial}.) We describe its classical form first. The $u$-side covers are generated by the classical Pieri rule, Theorem \ref{theorem:pierigeneral} at $q=0$ (the enumeration is Method \ref{method:qpieri} below with the quantum covers discarded); the $v$-side is the following signed path expansion.

The starting point of the $v$-side primitive is the structure of a \emph{dominant} double Schubert polynomial. If $\mu$ is dominant and the columns of its diagram have heights $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_m$ (so that $(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_m)=\code(\mu^{-1})$), then $\sch_\mu(x;z)$ factors as a product of factorial elementary symmetric polynomials of maximal degree, one per column of the shape,
$$\sch_\mu(x;z)=\prod_{i=1}^{m}E_{\lambda_i,\,\lambda_i}(x;z_i)$$
(Lemma \ref{lemma:dominantfactor}). For a general $v$ one writes $\sch_v(x;z)$ by applying divided differences in the $z$-variables to the dominant polynomial $\sch_{\mu^{-1}}(x;z)$ with $\mu=\dom(v^{-1})$, whose column heights are the parts of $\theta(v^{-1})$; because that polynomial factors by column, the divided-difference action decouples and may be carried out one column (one variable $z_i$) at a time. Applied to a single column this produces a \emph{signed} descending expansion. The following primitive carries it out.

\begin{definition}[$k$-strip descent beneath an edge, the down-Pieri problem] \label{def:kdownproblem}
Let $w,\mu\in S_n$ and $1\leq k<n$. Say $w'$ lies \emph{beneath the edge} $\mu$ if
$$\mathrm{inv}(w'\mu)=\mathrm{inv}(\mu)-\mathrm{inv}(w'),$$
that is, $\ell(w'\mu)=\ell(\mu)-\ell(w')$. A \emph{$k$-strip descent} of $w$ is a Bruhat descent $w\gtrdot wt_{ab}$ realized by a transposition $t_{ab}$ that moves the value in the pivot position $k$ either leftward (from a position $a<k$, with sign $-1$) or rightward (to a position $b>k$, with sign $+1$), touching only positions still holding their original value. Given a starting label $w$, the edge $\mu$, a degree $p$, and the column $k$, the \emph{down-Pieri problem} asks for the signed list of those length-$t$ descending $k$-strip chains from $w$ ($t\leq p$) whose endpoint lies beneath $\mu$.
\end{definition}

\begin{method}[$\textbf{\textsc{kdown}}(w,\mu,p,k)$] \label{method:kdown}
The algorithm returns a signed list $D$ of triples $(w',t,s)$, one for each length-$t$ descending $k$-strip chain from $w$ ($t\leq p$) whose endpoint $w'$ lies beneath $\mu$, with $s\in\{+1,-1\}$ its sign.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Initialize $D\gets[\,]$; if $w$ is already beneath $\mu$, append $(w,0,+1)$. Set a frontier $F\gets[(w,+1)]$.
\item For $t=1,\ldots,p$, form $F'\gets[\,]$ and, for each $(w'',s)\in F$:
  \begin{enumerate}
  \item (leftward) for each position $a<k$ with $w''(a)=w(a)$ such that $w''\gtrdot w''t_{a,k}$ is a Bruhat descent, set $w'''\gets w''t_{a,k}$ and append $(w''',-s)$ to $F'$; if $w'''$ is beneath $\mu$, append $(w''',t,-s)$ to $D$;
  \item (rightward) for each position $b>k$ with $w''(b)=w(b)$ such that $w''\gtrdot w''t_{k,b}$ is a Bruhat descent, set $w'''\gets w''t_{k,b}$ and append $(w''',+s)$ to $F'$; if $w'''$ is beneath $\mu$, append $(w''',t,+s)$ to $D$.
  \end{enumerate}
  Then set $F\gets F'$.
\item Return $D$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{method}

Applied without the edge $\mu$, the signed descending expansion produces many extraneous terms that ultimately cancel. The edge is exactly the bound that discards them: only endpoints beneath $\mu$ are retained in $D$. In the assembled $v$-side (Method~\ref{method:vpaths} below), only these retained labels seed the next column, so each stage passes forward only labels compatible with the remaining shape.

\begin{method}[$\textbf{\textsc{vpaths}}(v)$, the $v$-side path dictionaries] \label{method:vpaths}
Let $\theta:=\theta(v^{-1})$ with nonzero parts in columns $1,\ldots,L$, and let $\nu:=v\,\dom(v^{-1})$. Processing columns from $i=L$ down to $i=1$, maintain for each column a set of live labels (initialized at column $L$ to the single label $\nu$). At column $i$, with $k=i$ and edge $\mu_i:=\dom$ of the shape truncated below column $i$, call $\textbf{\textsc{kdown}}(w,\mu_i,\theta_i,k)$ for each live label $w$; record its signed output triples as the transitions out of $w$, and let their endpoints be the live labels seeding column $i-1$. The collected transitions form the path dictionaries used by $\textbf{\textsc{schubmult}}$.
\end{method}

\begin{method}[The algorithm \textbf{\textsc{schubmult}}] \label{method:schubmult}
Fix $n$ and let $u,v\in S_n$. The algorithm $\textbf{\textsc{schubmult}}(u,v)$ computes the expansion of $\sch_u(x;z)\,\sch_v(x;z)$ in the Schubert basis as follows. Set $\theta:=\theta(v^{-1})$ and let
$$L:=\#\{i\mid\theta_i\neq 0\}$$
be the number of nonzero parts. The algorithm proceeds in $L$ layers. It maintains a \emph{front}, a collection of nodes each consisting of an intermediate permutation on the $u$-side paired with a label on the $v$-side, initialized to the single node $(u,\,e)$. At layer $i$ it replaces each node by the nodes obtained by combining one $u$-side cover, generated by the classical Pieri rule, i.e.\ by $\textbf{\textsc{qpieri}}(\,\cdot\,,\theta_i,\theta_i)$ of Method~\ref{method:qpieri} below with the quantum covers discarded, with one $v$-side transition, generated by $\textbf{\textsc{vpaths}}(v)$ (Method~\ref{method:vpaths}), accumulating coefficients in the coefficient ring. After all $L$ layers the surviving coefficients at $v$-label $v\dom(v^{-1})$ give the structure constants.
\end{method}

\subsection{The quantum algorithm}
The same path organization gives the quantum multiplication algorithm, the routine \texttt{schubmult\_q\_double\_fast} of \cite{schubmult}. The $v$-side is unchanged except for the shape: the path dictionaries of Method~\ref{method:vpaths} are built over the columns of $\medtheta:=\medtheta(v^{-1})$ (Definition \ref{def:codetheta}), with $\nu:=v\,\medperm(v^{-1})$ in place of $v\dom(v^{-1})$, and by Theorem \ref{theorem:quandoublemed} they enumerate the chains $1=v_0\Tom1\cdots\Tom m v_m=\nu$ together with the data $\ell_i$, $\sigma_i$, $D_i$ of each step. On the $u$-side, the classical covers are replaced by the covers of the quantum Bruhat graph (Definition \ref{def:cover}, Remark~\ref{remark:qkbruhat}), including the length-decreasing moves, whose contributions carry the corresponding $q$-monomials. We first describe the two $u$-side primitives, which enumerate the chains of Theorem \ref{theorem:pierigeneral} and the compatible pairs of Corollary \ref{corollary:lowerdegrees}.

\begin{method}[$\textbf{\textsc{qpieri}}(u,p,k)$] \label{method:qpieri}
Given $u\in S_\infty$, $k\geq1$ and $0\leq p\leq k$, the algorithm returns a list $T$ of triples $(w,t,\mathbf q)$ with $w\in S_\infty$, $0\leq t\leq p$ and $\mathbf q$ a monomial in the $q_i$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Initialize $T\gets[(u,0,1)]$ and a frontier $F\gets[(u,1,\infty)]$ of triples $(w,\mathbf q,b_{\mathrm{last}})$.
\item For $t=1,\ldots,p$, form $F'\gets[\,]$ and, for each $(w,\mathbf q,b_{\mathrm{last}})\in F$:
  \begin{enumerate}
  \item compute the set $A\gets\{a\leq k:\ w(a)=u(a)\}$ of positions $\leq k$ not yet moved;
  \item for each $b$ with $k<b\leq b_{\mathrm{last}}$, in decreasing order, and each $a\in A$ such that $t_{ab}$ is a cover of $w$ (Definition \ref{def:cover}: either $w(a)<w(b)$ and no $w(r)$, $a<r<b$, lies in $(w(a),w(b))$, or $w(a)>w(b)$ and every $w(r)$, $a<r<b$, lies in $(w(b),w(a))$), put $\mathbf q'=\mathbf q\,\kappa(w,t_{ab})$, i.e.\ $\mathbf q'=\mathbf q$ for a classical cover and $\mathbf q'=\mathbf q\,q_{ab}=\mathbf q\,q_a\cdots q_{b-1}$ for a quantum cover, and append $(wt_{ab},\mathbf q',b)$ to $F'$ and $(wt_{ab},t,\mathbf q')$ to $T$.
  \end{enumerate}
  Then set $F\gets F'$.
\item Return $T$.
\end{enumerate}
Only finitely many $b$ qualify in (b): if $w$ fixes every position $>N$ then no $t_{ab}$ with $b>\max(N,k)+1$ is a cover of $w$ (the values $w(r)=r$ at positions $\max(N,k)<r<b$ lie between $w(a)$ and $w(b)=b$), so $b$ ranges over $k<b\leq\min(b_{\mathrm{last}},\max(N,k)+1)$. The cover test in (b) is performed numerically: the implementation computes the signed length change
$$\ell(wt_{ab})-\ell(w)=\pm1\pm2\,\#\{r\mid a<r<b,\ w(r)\text{ between }w(a)\text{ and }w(b)\}$$
of Lemma \ref{lemma:lengthchange} (the routine \texttt{count\_bruhat}, with the sign $+$ if $w(a)<w(b)$ and $-$ otherwise) and accepts $t_{ab}$ exactly when this number is $1$ or $1-2(b-a)$, multiplying the current monomial by $q_a\cdots q_{b-1}$ in the second case. This is the routine \texttt{elem\_sym\_perms\_q} of \cite{schubmult}; at $q=0$, i.e.\ discarding the quantum covers, it enumerates the chains of the classical Pieri rule of \cite{sottile,samuelmolev} (Theorem \ref{theorem:pierigeneral} at $q=0$).
\end{method}

\begin{lemma}[Correctness of \textbf{\textsc{qpieri}}] \label{lemma:qpieri}
The list $T$ returned by $\textbf{\textsc{qpieri}}(u,p,k)$ contains exactly one triple $(w,t,\mathbf q)$ for each $w\in S_\infty$ with $u\tom kw$ and $n_k(u,w)\leq p$, and for it $t=n_k(u,w)$ and $\mathbf q=q_k(u,w)$. Consequently, for $0\leq p'\leq p$,
$$\qsch_u(x;y)\,\qelem_{p',k}(x;z)=\sum_{(w,t,\mathbf q)\in T,\ t\leq p'}\mathbf q\,E_{p'-t,\,k-t}\big(y_{\fx_k(u,w)};z\big)\,\qsch_w(x;y),$$
where $\fx_k(u,w)=\{u(a):a\leq k,\ u(a)=w(a)\}$ is read off from $u$ and $w$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A triple $(w,t,\mathbf q)$ is appended to $T$ for each sequence of transpositions $t_{a_1b_1},\ldots,t_{a_tb_t}$ generated by the loop, and by construction these sequences are exactly those satisfying conditions (1)--(5) of Definition \ref{definition:pieri} with $p$ replaced by $t\leq p$: $a_i\leq k<b_i$, the $a_i$ are distinct (a position leaves $A$ once it is moved and, being moved only once, never returns, since $w(a)\neq u(a)$ after the move), the $b_i$ are weakly decreasing ($b\leq b_{\mathrm{last}}$), and each step is a cover (condition (4), by Definition \ref{def:cover}). The weight $\mathbf q$ is the product of the $\kappa$'s, which is $q_k(u,w)$ by the recursive definition in Definition \ref{definition:pieri}. It remains to see that each $w$ with $u\tom kw$ arises from exactly one such sequence. By Lemma \ref{lemma:piericycle}, a witnessing sequence is a pivot chain of $\pi=u^{-1}w$ in which the factors with a common $b$ are consecutive and the distinct values of $b$ decrease; the factors with a common $b$ form the pivot factorization \eqref{eq:pivot} of the cycle of $\pi$ with top $b$, whose order is determined by that cycle, and the order of the cycles is determined by their tops. Hence the sequence is determined by $w$, and $t=n_k(u,w)$ is its length. The displayed formula is Theorem \ref{theorem:pierigeneral} with $p'$ in place of $p$, the sum over $u\tom kw$ with $n_k(u,w)\leq p'$ being the sum over the triples with $t\leq p'$.
\end{proof}

\begin{method}[$\textbf{\textsc{doublepieri}}(u,p_1,p_2,k)$] \label{method:doublepieri}
Given $u\in S_\infty$, $k\geq1$ and $0\leq p_1,p_2\leq k$, the algorithm returns, for each triple $(v,t_1,\mathbf q_1)$ of $\textbf{\textsc{qpieri}}(u,p_1,k)$, a list $L(v,t_1,\mathbf q_1)$ of triples $(w,t_2,\mathbf q_2)$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Compute $T_1\gets\textbf{\textsc{qpieri}}(u,p_1,k)$.
\item For each $(v,t_1,\mathbf q_1)\in T_1$: compute the cycle decomposition of $\pi_1=u^{-1}v$, and record for each top $B$ the set $\mathrm{supp}\,c_B\setminus\{B\}$ of non-top elements of the cycle $c_B$ of $\pi_1$ with top $B$ (empty if there is none); compute $T_2\gets\textbf{\textsc{qpieri}}(v,p_2,k)$; and for each $(w,t_2,\mathbf q_2)\in T_2$ compute the cycles of $\pi_2=v^{-1}w$ and append $(w,t_2,\mathbf q_2)$ to $L(v,t_1,\mathbf q_1)$ unless some cycle $d$ of $\pi_2$ has a non-top element in $\mathrm{supp}\,c_{\max d}\setminus\{\max d\}$.
\item Return the lists $L$.
\end{enumerate}
This is the routine \texttt{double\_elem\_sym\_q} of \cite{schubmult}.
\end{method}

\begin{lemma}[Correctness of \textbf{\textsc{doublepieri}}] \label{lemma:doublepieri}
The pairs $\big((v,t_1,\mathbf q_1),(w,t_2,\mathbf q_2)\big)$ with $(w,t_2,\mathbf q_2)\in L(v,t_1,\mathbf q_1)$ returned by $\textbf{\textsc{doublepieri}}(u,p_1,p_2,k)$ are in bijection with the compatible pairs $u\tom kv\tom kw$ with $n_k(u,v)\leq p_1$ and $n_k(v,w)\leq p_2$, with $t_1=n_k(u,v)$, $t_2=n_k(v,w)$, $\mathbf q_1\mathbf q_2=q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w)$. Consequently, for $0\leq p_1'\leq p_1$ and $0\leq p_2'\leq p_2$ and two alphabets $z,z'$,
$$\qsch_u(x;y)\,\qelem_{p_1',p_2';k}(x;z,z')=\sum_{\substack{(w,t_2,\mathbf q_2)\in L(v,t_1,\mathbf q_1)\\ t_1\leq p_1',\ t_2\leq p_2'}}\mathbf q_1\mathbf q_2\,E_{p_1'-t_1,\,k-t_1}\big(y_{\fx_k(u,v)};z\big)\,E_{p_2'-t_2,\,k-t_2}\big(y_{\fx_k(v,w)};z'\big)\,\qsch_w(x;y).$$
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By Lemma \ref{lemma:qpieri} the pairs examined in step 2 are in bijection with the pairs of chains $u\tom kv\tom kw$ with $n_k(u,v)\leq p_1$, $n_k(v,w)\leq p_2$, with the stated $t$'s and $\mathbf q$'s. The rejection test is the negation of Definition \ref{def:compatible}: a cycle $d$ of $v^{-1}w$ whose top $\max d$ is the top of a cycle $c$ of $u^{-1}v$ must satisfy $c\cap d=\{\max d\}$, i.e.\ no non-top element of $d$ may lie in $c$ (it cannot equal $\max d=\max c$), and cycles of $v^{-1}w$ whose top is not a top of $u^{-1}v$ are unrestricted. The formula is Corollary \ref{corollary:lowerdegrees} with $(p_1',p_2')$ in place of $(p_1,p_2)$.
\end{proof}

The implementation calls $\textbf{\textsc{qpieri}}$ and $\textbf{\textsc{doublepieri}}$ once per front node with $p$, respectively $(p_1,p_2)$, equal to the largest degrees $k-\ell_i$, respectively $(k-\ell_i,k-\ell_{i+1})$, occurring among the $v$-side transitions of the layer, and then evaluates the coefficient of each transition by the displayed formulas with the actual degrees $p'\leq p$ (the routine \texttt{elem\_sym\_func\_q}, which reads $\fx_k(u,w)$ off the positions $a\leq k$ with $u(a)=w(a)$ and the alphabet $z_{D_i}$ off the transition); by the two lemmas this is the same as calling them separately for each transition.

\begin{method}[The algorithm \textbf{\textsc{schubmult}}$_q$] \label{method:schubmultq}
Fix $n$ and $u,v\in S_n$, and let $\medtheta=\medtheta(v^{-1})$ with nonzero parts $\medtheta_1\geq\cdots\geq\medtheta_m>0$, paired indices $\mathcal P$ and single indices $\mathcal S$ as in Theorem \ref{theorem:quandoublemed}. The algorithm computes the expansion of $\qsch_u(x;y)\,\qsch_v(x;z)$ in the basis $\{\qsch_w(x;y)\}$. It maintains a front of nodes $(w,\lambda)$, a $u$-side permutation paired with a $v$-side label, initialized to $(u,e)$ with coefficient $1$, and runs through the indices $i=1,\ldots,m$, skipping $i\in\mathcal P+1$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $i\in\mathcal S$, with $k=\medtheta_i$: for each node $(w,\lambda)$ and each $v$-side transition $\lambda\to\lambda'$ of column $i$, with data $(\ell_i,\sigma_i,D_i)$, and each $(w',t,\mathbf q)\in\textbf{\textsc{qpieri}}(w,k-\ell_i,k)$, create the node $(w',\lambda')$ with coefficient
$$(-1)^{\sigma_i}\,\mathbf q\,E_{k-\ell_i-t,\,k-t}\big(y_{\fx_k(w,w')};z_{D_i}\big)$$
times the coefficient of $(w,\lambda)$; by Lemma \ref{lemma:qpieri} this expands $\qsch_w(x;y)\,\qelem_{k-\ell_i,k}(x;z_{D_i})$.
\item If $i\in\mathcal P$, with $k=\medtheta_i=\medtheta_{i+1}$: for each node $(w,\lambda)$ and each pair of consecutive $v$-side transitions $\lambda\to\lambda'\to\lambda''$ of the columns $i$ and $i+1$, with data $(\ell_i,\sigma_i,D_i)$ and $(\ell_{i+1},\sigma_{i+1},D_{i+1})$, and each pair $\big((w',t_1,\mathbf q_1),(w'',t_2,\mathbf q_2)\big)$ returned by $\textbf{\textsc{doublepieri}}(w,k-\ell_i,k-\ell_{i+1},k)$, create the node $(w'',\lambda'')$ with coefficient
$$(-1)^{\sigma_i+\sigma_{i+1}}\,\mathbf q_1\mathbf q_2\,E_{k-\ell_i-t_1,\,k-t_1}\big(y_{\fx_k(w,w')};z_{D_i}\big)\,E_{k-\ell_{i+1}-t_2,\,k-t_2}\big(y_{\fx_k(w',w'')};z_{D_{i+1}}\big)$$
