By Lemma \ref{lemma:merge} applied to $(\omega_a,D_a)$: a fixed-point candidate contributes only if $a=k-1$, i.e.\ $D=\{k-1\}$; and if $D_a=D$ is nontrivial, at most one $a\in D$ contributes, and one does if and only if $D$ is $\omega_a$-tight, where now $\omega_a=u\rho C=:\omega$. Altogether at most one summand of type (M) is nonzero, and one is if and only if $C$ is $u\rho$-tight and either $D=\{k-1\}$, or $D$ is nontrivial with $\max D=k-1$ and $\omega$-tight. This is exactly the condition for $u\tom{k-2}w$: $\pi=\rho CD$ is $(k-2)$-shaped (as $k-1\notin C$, $C$ has the unique element $k$ greater than $k-2$, and $D$ the unique element $k-1$) and it is $u$-tight if and only if $C$ is $u\rho$-tight and $D$ is $\omega$-tight. When it holds, Lemma \ref{lemma:merge}(c) gives
$$\kappa_{ak}q_{k-1}(u,wt_{ak})=\qw_u(\rho)\qw_u(C)\,\kappa_{ak}\qw_\omega(\tau_a)=\qw_u(\rho)\qw_u(C)\,q_{k-1}\qw_\omega(D)=q_{k-1}q_{k-2}(u,w),$$
and $\fx_{k-1}(u,wt_{ak})=\fx_{k-1}(u,w)\setminus\{u(a)\}=\fx_{k-2}(u,w)$ as in the proof of Lemma \ref{lemma:reflectioncancel}. Thus the summand of type (M) equals $-q_{k-1}c_{k-2}(u,w)$ and cancels the quantum term; when the condition fails, both vanish.
\end{proof}

We now record the recurrence satisfied by the quantum factorial elementary polynomials, which drives the induction.

\begin{lemma} \label{lemma:elemrecursion}
For all integers $k$ we have
$$\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)=(x_k-z_1)\qelem_{k-1,k-1}(x;z)+q_{k-1}\qelem_{k-2,k-2}(x;z),$$
with the conventions $\qelem_{0,0}=1$ and $\qelem_{k,k}=0$ if $k<0$. Moreover, the ordinary polynomials $\wte(y_A;z)=\prod_{i\in A}(y_i-z_1)$ satisfy, for any set $A$ and any $c\notin A$, the single-variable identity
$$\wte(y_{A\cup\{c\}};z)=(y_c-z_1)\,\wte(y_A;z).$$
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By Lemma \ref{lemma:qelemkk}, $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)=\Delta_k(-z_1\mid x_1,\ldots,x_k)$, and expanding the tridiagonal determinant $\Delta_k(t\mid x_1,\ldots,x_k)$ along its last row gives $\Delta_k=(x_k+t)\Delta_{k-1}+q_{k-1}\Delta_{k-2}$. The second identity is clear.
\end{proof}

The first recurrence is the quantization of the classical relation $E_{k,k}=(x_k-z_1)E_{k-1,k-1}$; the extra term $q_{k-1}\qelem_{k-2,k-2}$ is the only feature absent from the non-quantum setting. Discarding all quantum terms recovers the classical Pieri formula \cite[Theorem~7.1]{samuelmolev}, whose term-by-term cancellation is carried out in \cite[Proof of Proposition~4.4]{samuelmolev} by means of the reflection-matching Lemmas 7.6--7.9 of that paper; the lemmas above are the quantum counterparts, the essential new points being the length-decreasing covers permitted by Lemma \ref{lemma:piericycle}, the attendant bookkeeping of the powers of $q$, and the extra term of the recurrence, which is absorbed by the merge summands of Lemma \ref{lemma:merge}.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem \ref{theorem:pieri}]
For a permutation $w$, write $[\qsch_w]F$ for the coefficient of $\qsch_w(x;y)$ in an expansion $F$ of a quantum double Schubert polynomial in the basis $\{\qsch_w(x;y)\}$. With this notation the theorem is the assertion that, for all $u$ and all $k\geq1$,
$$[\qsch_w]\big(\qsch_u(x;y)\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)\big)=\begin{cases}q_k(u,w)\,\wte(y_{\fx_{k}(u,w)};z) & \text{if }u\tom{k} w,\\[2pt] 0 & \text{otherwise.}\end{cases}$$
For brevity, when $u\tom{m} w$ we set
$$c_m(u,w)=q_m(u,w)\,\wte(y_{\fx_{m}(u,w)};z),$$
and $c_m(u,w)=0$ if $u\ntom{m}w$; note that $u\tom{0}w$ only for $w=u$, with $c_0(u,u)=1$, in accordance with $\qelem_{0,0}=1$. The claim is that $[\qsch_w](\qsch_u\qelem_{k,k})=c_k(u,w)$.

We argue by strong induction on $k$.

\emph{Base case $k=1$.} We have $\qelem_{1,1}(x;z)=x_1-z_1$, and Lemma \ref{lemma:singlevariable} with $i=j=1$ gives
$$(x_1-z_1)\qsch_u(x;y)=(y_{u(1)}-z_1)\qsch_u(x;y)+\sum_{\substack{b>1\\\ell(ut_{1b})=\ell(u)+1}}\qsch_{ut_{1b}}(x;y)+\sum_{\substack{b>1\\\ell(ut_{1b})=\ell(u)-2(b-1)+1}}q_{1b}\qsch_{ut_{1b}}(x;y).$$
The permutations $w$ with $u\tom{1}w$ are exactly $w=u$ and $w=ut_{1b}$ for $b>1$ with $u\lup ut_{1b}$, since Definition \ref{definition:pieri} permits at most one reflection when $k=1$. For $w=u$ we have $q_1=1$, $\fx_{1}(u,u)=\{u(1)\}$, and $\wte(y_{\{u(1)\}};z)=y_{u(1)}-z_1$, matching the diagonal term. For $w=ut_{1b}$ we have $\fx_{1}=\emptyset$ and $\wte(y_\emptyset;z)=1$; if the step is length-increasing then $q_1(u,w)=1$, matching the second sum, and if it is length-decreasing then $q_1(u,w)=q_{1b}$, matching the third sum. All other coefficients vanish on both sides. This proves the base case.

\emph{Inductive step.} Fix $k>1$ and assume the claim at all levels $m<k$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:elemrecursion},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pieriexpand}
\qsch_u\qelem_{k,k}=(x_k-z_1)\big(\qsch_u\qelem_{k-1,k-1}\big)+q_{k-1}\,\qsch_u\qelem_{k-2,k-2}.
\end{equation}
By the inductive hypothesis, $\qsch_u\qelem_{k-1,k-1}=\sum_v c_{k-1}(u,v)\,\qsch_v$. Applying Lemma \ref{lemma:singlevariable} with $i=k$, $j=1$ to each $\qsch_v$ and extracting the coefficient of $\qsch_w$ from \eqref{eq:pieriexpand}, we obtain
\begin{align}
[\qsch_w]\big(\qsch_u\qelem_{k,k}\big)
&=(y_{w(k)}-z_1)\,c_{k-1}(u,w) \label{eq:diag}\\
&\quad+\sum_{\substack{b>k,\ w t_{kb}\lup w\\ \ell(w)=\ell(wt_{kb})+1}} c_{k-1}(u,wt_{kb})
+\sum_{\substack{b>k,\ w t_{kb}\lup w\\ \ell(w)=\ell(wt_{kb})-2(b-k)+1}} q_{kb}\,c_{k-1}(u,wt_{kb}) \label{eq:up}\\
&\quad-\sum_{\substack{a<k,\ w t_{ak}\lup w\\ \ell(w)=\ell(wt_{ak})+1}} c_{k-1}(u,wt_{ak})
-\sum_{\substack{a<k,\ w t_{ak}\lup w\\ \ell(w)=\ell(wt_{ak})-2(k-a)+1}} q_{ak}\,c_{k-1}(u,wt_{ak}) \label{eq:down}\\
&\quad+q_{k-1}\,c_{k-2}(u,w), \label{eq:rec}
\end{align}
where we used that $\mathrm{sign}(b-k)=1$ for $b>k$ and $\mathrm{sign}(a-k)=-1$ for $a<k$, that the term $\qsch_w$ arises from $\qsch_v$ with $v=wt$ exactly when $v\lup w$, and that a summand in \eqref{eq:up}--\eqref{eq:down} is present precisely when $u\tom{k-1}wt_{kb}$, respectively $u\tom{k-1}wt_{ak}$ (otherwise $c_{k-1}=0$). In the terminology introduced before Lemma \ref{lemma:neighbors}, \eqref{eq:up} and \eqref{eq:down} are the Monk summands at $w$, with $\kappa_{kb}$, respectively $\kappa_{ak}$, equal to $1$ in the first sum and to $q_{kb}$, respectively $q_{ak}$, in the second. We must show that \eqref{eq:diag}--\eqref{eq:rec} equals $c_k(u,w)$. We distinguish three cases according to whether $u(k)=w(k)$ and, when $u(k)\neq w(k)$, whether the cycle $C$ of $u^{-1}w$ containing $k$ has its top $B$ greater than $k$ or equal to $k$.

\emph{Case 1: $u(k)=w(k)$.} By Lemma \ref{lemma:leveldichotomy}(i) we have $u\tom{k}w$ if and only if $u\tom{k-1}w$; suppose first that both hold. Then, by the same lemma,
$$\fx_{k}(u,w)=\fx_{{k-1}}(u,w)\cup\{u(k)\},\qquad q_k(u,w)=q_{k-1}(u,w),$$
so the diagonal contribution \eqref{eq:diag} gives
$$(y_{u(k)}-z_1)\,c_{k-1}(u,w)=q_{k-1}(u,w)\,(y_{u(k)}-z_1)\,\wte(y_{\fx_{{k-1}}(u,w)};z)=q_k(u,w)\,\wte(y_{\fx_{k}(u,w)};z)=c_k(u,w),$$
by Lemma \ref{lemma:elemrecursion}. The remaining contributions are the reflection sums \eqref{eq:up}, \eqref{eq:down} and the quantum term $q_{k-1}c_{k-2}(u,w)$ of \eqref{eq:rec}, which sum to $0$ by Lemma \ref{lemma:reflectioncancel}. Hence the total is $c_k(u,w)$, as required. If instead $u(k)=w(k)$ but $u\ntom{k}w$ (equivalently $u\ntom{k-1}w$), then $c_k(u,w)=0$ and the diagonal term vanishes, while the reflection sums and quantum term again cancel by Lemma \ref{lemma:reflectioncancel}; the total is $0=c_k(u,w)$.

\emph{Case 2: $u(k)\neq w(k)$ and $B>k$.} By Lemma \ref{lemma:movedcancel}(a), the diagonal term \eqref{eq:diag} and the quantum term \eqref{eq:rec} vanish, and the Monk summands \eqref{eq:up}--\eqref{eq:down} sum to $c_k(u,w)$. (When $u\tom{k}w$, Lemma \ref{lemma:pieriknotkn1} identifies the single surviving summand: it is indexed by $t_{kB}$, with $v=wt_{kB}$, $u\tom{k-1}v$, $\fx_k(u,w)=\fx_{k-1}(u,v)$ and $\kappa_{kB}\,q_{k-1}(u,v)=q_k(u,w)$, the factor $\kappa_{kB}$ being $1$ or $q_{kB}$ according as $t_{kB}$ is a classical or a quantum cover of $v$; this is the step at which the quantum drop terms of Lemma \ref{lemma:singlevariable} and the quantum weight of Definition \ref{definition:pieri} interlock.)

\emph{Case 3: $u(k)\neq w(k)$ and $B=k$.} Here $u\ntom{k}w$, since $C$ contains no element greater than $k$, so $c_k(u,w)=0$; and \eqref{eq:diag}--\eqref{eq:rec} vanishes by Lemma \ref{lemma:movedcancel}(b).

By Lemma \ref{lemma:leveldichotomy} these cases are exhaustive. In all cases $[\qsch_w](\qsch_u\qelem_{k,k})=c_k(u,w)$, completing the induction and the proof.
\end{proof}

\section{Proofs of the expansion formulas} \label{section:proofvside}

In this section we prove Theorems \ref{theorem:orddouble}, \ref{theorem:quandouble} and \ref{theorem:quandoublemed}. Throughout, divided difference operators act on the $z$-variables, and in order to free the subscript for the skew operators we write
$$\partial^a=\partial^{(z)}_a,\qquad \partial^w=\partial^{(z)}_w=\partial^{a_1}\cdots\partial^{a_m}\quad(w=s_{a_1}\cdots s_{a_m}\text{ reduced}),$$
the rightmost operator being applied first. The simple reflection $s_a$ acts on polynomials by exchanging $z_a$ and $z_{a+1}$, and a permutation $w$ acts by $w(z_i)=z_{w(i)}$, so that $w=s_{a_1}\circ\cdots\circ s_{a_m}$ as operators. All operators of this section are linear over $\mathbb Z[q][x]$, which we treat as the ring of scalars. Recall that the quantization map $\mathcal Q$ of Fomin, Gelfand and Postnikov (Lemma \ref{lemma:quantumtoordinary}) is linear over $\mathbb Z[q][z]$, sends every standard elementary monomial $\prod_je_{i_j}(x_1,\ldots,x_j)$ to $\prod_je^{(q)}_{i_j}(x_1,\ldots,x_j)$, and satisfies $\mathcal Q(\sch_w(x;z))=\qsch_w(x;z)$.

\subsection{Skew divided difference operators}

We use the skew divided difference operators of \cite{samuelleibniz,samuelmolev}. They are the operators $\partial^w_u$, $u,w\in S_\infty$, determined by the Leibniz formula
\begin{equation}\label{eq:leibniz2}
\partial^w(PQ)=\sum_{u\in S_\infty}\partial^u(P)\,\partial^w_u(Q),
\end{equation}
which differs by a normalization from the convention of Macdonald \cite{notes}. We shall use the following facts.

\begin{lemma} \label{lemma:skewfacts}
Let $u,w\in S_\infty$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] (Subword formula.) If $(a_1,\ldots,a_m)$ is a reduced word for $w$, then $\partial^w_u$ is the sum, over all reduced subwords $(b_1,\ldots,b_m)$ for $u$ (that is, $b_j\in\{1,s_{a_j}\}$ for each $j$, and the nonunit entries form a reduced word for $u$), of the compositions $\partial^{a_1}_{b_1}\cdots\partial^{a_m}_{b_m}$, where $\partial^{a}_{1}=\partial^a$ and $\partial^{a}_{s_a}=s_a$.
\item[(ii)] $\partial^w_u=0$ unless $u\leq w$ in Bruhat order; $\partial^w_1=\partial^w$ and $\partial^w_w=w$; and $\partial^w_u$ is homogeneous of degree $\ell(u)-\ell(w)$ in the $z$-variables. In particular, for a scalar $c$, $\partial^w_u(c)=c$ if $u=w$ and $\partial^w_u(c)=0$ otherwise.
\item[(iii)] (Ascending Leibniz formula.) For polynomials $f_1,\ldots,f_k$,
$$\partial^w_u(f_1f_2\cdots f_k)=\sum_{u=u_0\leq u_1\leq\cdots\leq u_k=w}\ \prod_{j=1}^k\partial^{u_j}_{u_{j-1}}(f_j).$$
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(i) is proved in \cite{samuelmolev} (it goes back to Macdonald \cite{notes}). (ii) follows from (i): a reduced subword for $u$ of a reduced word for $w$ exists only if $u\leq w$; the only subword for $u=1$ is the empty one and the only one for $u=w$ is the full word, whose composition $s_{a_1}\circ\cdots\circ s_{a_m}$ is the action of $w$; and each composition in (i) consists of $\ell(u)$ reflections, which preserve degree, and $\ell(w)-\ell(u)$ divided differences, which lower it by one. (iii) is a rearrangement of the generalized Leibniz rule of \cite[Corollary~3.5.4]{samuelleibniz}: in the notation of that work $\partial^w_u=\sum_vc^w_{u,v}\partial_v$, the coefficients $c^w_{u_0,u_1,\ldots,u_k}$ of the $k$-fold rule factor as $\sum_{v_1,\ldots,v_{k-1}}c^{v_1}_{u_0,u_1}c^{v_2}_{v_1,u_2}\cdots c^{w}_{v_{k-1},u_k}$ by associativity, and $\sum_{u_j}c^{v_j}_{v_{j-1},u_j}\partial_{u_j}(f_j)=\partial^{v_j}_{v_{j-1}}(f_j)$. By (ii) only chains $u_0\leq u_1\leq\cdots\leq u_k$ contribute.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Linear forms] \label{lemma:skewlinear}
Let $u,w\in S_\infty$ and $i\geq1$. Then
$$\partial^w_u(z_i)=\begin{cases}z_{u(i)}&\text{if }w=u,\\ 1&\text{if }w=ut_{ib}\text{ with }b>i\text{ and }\ell(w)=\ell(u)+1,\\ -1&\text{if }w=ut_{bi}\text{ with }b<i\text{ and }\ell(w)=\ell(u)+1,\\ 0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}$$
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The case $w=u$ is Lemma \ref{lemma:skewfacts}(ii), and by the same lemma $\partial^w_u(z_i)=0$ unless $u<w$ and $\ell(w)=\ell(u)+1$. So let $w=ut_{ab}$ with $a<b$ and $\ell(w)=\ell(u)+1$, so that $u(a)<u(b)$. Fix a reduced word $(a_1,\ldots,a_m)$ for $w$ and put $\sigma_p=s_{a_{p+1}}\cdots s_{a_m}$. The reduced subwords for $u$ omit exactly one letter, and omitting the $p$-th letter gives the element $w\,\sigma_p^{-1}s_{a_p}\sigma_p=w\,t_{\sigma_p^{-1}(a_p),\,\sigma_p^{-1}(a_p+1)}$. The reflections $\sigma_p^{-1}s_{a_p}\sigma_p$, $1\leq p\leq m$, are pairwise distinct (they are the $m$ reflections $t$ with $\ell(wt)<\ell(w)$, see \cite[Section~1.6--1.7]{humphreys1992reflection}), so exactly one $p$ yields $u$, namely the one with $\{\sigma_p^{-1}(a_p),\sigma_p^{-1}(a_p+1)\}=\{a,b\}$, and by Lemma \ref{lemma:skewfacts}(i)
$$\partial^w_u(z_i)=s_{a_1}\cdots s_{a_{p-1}}\,\partial^{a_p}\,\sigma_p(z_i)=s_{a_1}\cdots s_{a_{p-1}}\,\partial^{a_p}\big(z_{\sigma_p(i)}\big),$$
which is $1$ if $\sigma_p(i)=a_p$, $-1$ if $\sigma_p(i)=a_p+1$, and $0$ otherwise. It remains to see that $\sigma_p^{-1}(a_p)=a$ and $\sigma_p^{-1}(a_p+1)=b$. The word $(a_1,\ldots,a_p)$ is reduced and ends with $a_p$, so $\tau=s_{a_1}\cdots s_{a_p}$ satisfies $\tau(a_p)>\tau(a_p+1)$; since $w=\tau\sigma_p$ this says $w(\sigma_p^{-1}(a_p))>w(\sigma_p^{-1}(a_p+1))$. As $w(a)=u(b)>u(a)=w(b)$, the claim follows.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Factorial elementary polynomials and dominant permutations}

\begin{lemma} \label{lemma:Ehe}
For $0\leq p\leq k$,
\begin{align*}
E_{p,k}(x;z)&=\sum_{m=0}^{p}(-1)^{p-m}h_{p-m}(z_1,\ldots,z_{k-p+1})\,e_m(x_1,\ldots,x_k),\\
\qelem_{p,k}(x;z)&=\sum_{m=0}^{p}(-1)^{p-m}h_{p-m}(z_1,\ldots,z_{k-p+1})\,e^{(q)}_m(x_1,\ldots,x_k).
\end{align*}
In particular $E_{p,k}(x;z)$ and $\qelem_{p,k}(x;z)$ are symmetric in $z_1,\ldots,z_{k-p+1}$, and $\qelem_{p,k}(x;z)=\mathcal Q\big(E_{p,k}(x;z)\big)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Lemma \ref{lemma:quantumtoordinary} with $y=0$ gives $\qsch_w(x;z)=\sum_{v_1\cdot v_2=w}\sch_{v_1}(0;z)\qsch_{v_2}(x)$. The permutation $c[p,k]=s_{k-p+1}\cdots s_k$ has a unique reduced word, so its reduced factorizations are $v_1=s_{k-p+1}\cdots s_j$, $v_2=s_{j+1}\cdots s_k$ with $k-p\leq j\leq k$. Here $v_2$ is the Grassmannian permutation with descent at $k$ whose diagram is a single column of height $m=k-j$, so $\sch_{v_2}(x)=e_m(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$ and $\qsch_{v_2}(x)=e^{(q)}_m(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$ \cite{fomin1997quantum}; and $\sch_{v_1}(0;z)=(-1)^{\ell(v_1)}\sch_{v_1^{-1}}(z)$, where $v_1^{-1}=s_j\cdots s_{k-p+1}$ is the Grassmannian permutation with descent at $k-p+1$ whose diagram is a single row of length $\ell(v_1)=p-m$, so that $\sch_{v_1^{-1}}(z)=h_{p-m}(z_1,\ldots,z_{k-p+1})$. Summing over $j$ gives the second formula, and the first is its specialization at $q=0$. The symmetry is clear from the formulas, and the last assertion holds because $\mathcal Q$ fixes $\mathbb Z[z]$ and sends the standard elementary monomial $e_m(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$ to $e^{(q)}_m(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Dominant permutations] \label{lemma:dominantfactor}
Let $\mu$ be a dominant permutation with $\code(\mu)=\theta$, $\theta_1\geq\cdots\geq\theta_k>0$. Then $\mu^{-1}$ is dominant, the $i$-th column of its diagram has height $\theta_i$, and
$$\sch_{\mu^{-1}}(x;z)=\prod_{i=1}^kE_{\theta_i,\theta_i}(x;z_i)=\prod_{i=1}^k\prod_{j=1}^{\theta_i}(x_j-z_i).$$
If moreover $\theta_1>\cdots>\theta_k$, then $\qsch_{\mu^{-1}}(x;z)=\prod_{i=1}^k\qelem_{\theta_i,\theta_i}(x;z_i)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A permutation is dominant if and only if its diagram is the Young diagram of its code, placed in the upper left corner, and for such a permutation $\rho$ one has $\sch_\rho(x;z)=\prod_{(a,b)\in D(\rho)}(x_a-z_b)$ \cite{notes}. The diagram of $\mu^{-1}$ is the transpose of that of $\mu$, so $D(\mu^{-1})=\{(j,i)\mid 1\leq i\leq k,\ 1\leq j\leq\theta_i\}$, which is again a Young diagram in the upper left corner; this gives the first assertions and the factorization. For the quantum statement, expand each factor by Lemma \ref{lemma:qelemkk} at $q=0$: $E_{\theta_i,\theta_i}(x;z_i)=\sum_{m}(-z_i)^{\theta_i-m}e_m(x_1,\ldots,x_{\theta_i})$. Multiplying out, each term $\prod_ie_{m_i}(x_1,\ldots,x_{\theta_i})$ is a standard elementary monomial because the alphabets $x_1,\ldots,x_{\theta_i}$ have pairwise distinct lengths, and $\mathcal Q$ sends it to $\prod_ie^{(q)}_{m_i}(x_1,\ldots,x_{\theta_i})$. Hence $\qsch_{\mu^{-1}}(x;z)=\mathcal Q(\sch_{\mu^{-1}}(x;z))=\prod_i\sum_m(-z_i)^{\theta_i-m}e^{(q)}_m(x_1,\ldots,x_{\theta_i})=\prod_i\qelem_{\theta_i,\theta_i}(x;z_i)$ by Lemma \ref{lemma:qelemkk}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The skew divided differences of $E_{k,k}(x;z_i)$}

For $i\geq1$ and an integer $b\neq i$ put $\epsilon_i(b)=1$ if $b<i$ and $\epsilon_i(b)=-1$ if $b>i$. If $u\Tom{i}w$ with chain $b_1,\ldots,b_k$ as in Definition \ref{def:itom}, then $\prod_j\epsilon_i(b_j)=(-1)^{\ell(u,w)-\sigma_i(u,w)}$. The heart of the matter is the following lemma, which governs the cancellation of the chains through position $i$ that revisit a position.

\begin{lemma}[Rotation] \label{lemma:rotation}
Let $\psi\in S_\infty$, $i\geq1$, and let $\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_m$ ($m\geq2$) be distinct integers different from $i$, with indices read modulo $m$. For $1\leq r\leq m$ let
$$R_r=(\gamma_r,\gamma_{r+1},\ldots,\gamma_{r+m-1},\gamma_r),$$
a sequence of length $m+1$, and call $R_r$ \emph{valid} if the sequence of permutations $\psi,\ \psi t_{i\gamma_r},\ \psi t_{i\gamma_r}t_{i\gamma_{r+1}},\ \ldots$ obtained by applying its entries in turn is a chain of Bruhat covers. Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $t_{i\gamma_r}t_{i\gamma_{r+1}}\cdots t_{i\gamma_{r+m-1}}t_{i\gamma_r}=C$ for every $r$, where $C$ is the cycle $\gamma_j\mapsto\gamma_{j-1}$ ($1\leq j\leq m$), which fixes $i$. In particular all valid $R_r$ lead from $\psi$ to the same permutation $\psi C$.
\item[(ii)] Listed in cyclic order of $r$, the valid $R_r$ alternate between those with $\gamma_r<i$ and those with $\gamma_r>i$. In particular the number of valid $R_r$ with $\gamma_r<i$ equals the number of valid $R_r$ with $\gamma_r>i$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(i) Since $t_{ib}t_{ic}t_{ib}=t_{bc}$ for distinct $b,c\neq i$, the product equals $t_{\gamma_r\gamma_{r+1}}t_{\gamma_r\gamma_{r+2}}\cdots t_{\gamma_r\gamma_{r+m-1}}$, which in the notation \eqref{eq:pivot} is the cycle $(\gamma_{r+m-1},\ldots,\gamma_{r+1},\gamma_r)$, sending $\gamma_{r+m-1}\mapsto\gamma_{r+m-2}\mapsto\cdots\mapsto\gamma_{r+1}\mapsto\gamma_r\mapsto\gamma_{r+m-1}$.

(ii) Put $c=\psi(i)$ and $v_j=\psi(\gamma_j)$. Fix $r$ and follow $R_r$. Before the $s$-th step ($1\leq s\leq m$) the position $i$ carries $v_{r+s-2}$ (that is, $c$ when $s=1$), and the position $\gamma_{r+s-1}$, which has not been moved yet, carries $v_{r+s-1}$; before the last step the position $i$ carries $v_{r-1}$ and the position $\gamma_r$ carries $c$. Call a step exchanging the position $i$ with a position $g$ \emph{ascending} if the value at $g$ exceeds the value at $i$ when $g>i$, and is smaller than it when $g<i$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:lengthchange} an ascending step increases the length by at least $1$, a non-ascending step decreases it, and a step is a Bruhat cover exactly when it is ascending and increases the length by exactly $1$. The total change in length along $R_r$ is $\ell(\psi C)-\ell(\psi)$ by (i), independently of $r$. Consequently $R_r$ is valid if and only if all of its $m+1$ steps are ascending and $\ell(\psi C)-\ell(\psi)=m+1$.

By the description of the values before each step, the steps $2,\ldots,m$ of $R_r$ are all ascending if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:DC}
v_j>v_{j-1}\iff\gamma_j>i\qquad\text{for all }j\neq r,
\end{equation}
while the first step is ascending if and only if ($v_r>c\iff\gamma_r>i$) and the last if and only if ($c>v_{r-1}\iff\gamma_r>i$). The last two conditions together say precisely that $c$ lies strictly between $v_{r-1}$ and $v_r$ and that \eqref{eq:DC} holds for $j=r$ as well. Therefore $R_r$ is valid if and only if
$$\ell(\psi C)-\ell(\psi)=m+1,\qquad\text{\eqref{eq:DC} holds for all }j,\qquad\text{and }c\text{ lies strictly between }v_{r-1}\text{ and }v_r.$$
The first two conditions do not involve $r$. If they hold, the valid indices $r$ are exactly those at which the cyclic sequence $v_1,\ldots,v_m$, none of whose terms equals $c$, crosses the level $c$; and consecutive crossings of a cyclic sequence are alternately upward ($v_{r-1}<c<v_r$) and downward ($v_{r-1}>c>v_r$). By \eqref{eq:DC} the upward crossings are those with $\gamma_r>i$ and the downward ones those with $\gamma_r<i$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem} \label{theorem:skewtop}
Let $u,w\in S_\infty$, $i\geq1$ and $k\geq0$. Then $\partial^w_u\big(E_{k,k}(x;z_i)\big)=0$ unless $u\Tom{i}w$, in which case
$$\partial^w_u\big(E_{k,k}(x;z_i)\big)=(-1)^{\ell(u,w)-\sigma_i(u,w)}\,E_{k-\ell(u,w),\,k}\big(x;z_{D_i(u,w)}\big).$$
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $E_{k,k}(x;z_i)=f_1\cdots f_k$ with $f_j=x_j-z_i$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:skewfacts}(iii),
$$\partial^w_u\big(E_{k,k}(x;z_i)\big)=\sum_{u=u_0\leq\cdots\leq u_k=w}\ \prod_{j=1}^k\partial^{u_j}_{u_{j-1}}(x_j-z_i),$$
and by Lemmas \ref{lemma:skewlinear} and \ref{lemma:skewfacts}(ii) the factor $\partial^{u_j}_{u_{j-1}}(x_j-z_i)$ equals $x_j-z_{u_j(i)}$ if $u_j=u_{j-1}$, equals $\epsilon_i(b)$ if $u_j=u_{j-1}t_{ib}$ with $\ell(u_j)=\ell(u_{j-1})+1$, and vanishes otherwise. Call a sequence $(u_0,\ldots,u_k)$ in which every step is of one of these two kinds an \emph{$i$-walk} from $u$ to $w$. Thus the sum is over the $i$-walks, and a walk contributes the product of $x_j-z_{u_j(i)}$ over its trivial steps and of $\epsilon_i(b)$ over its nontrivial steps.

An \emph{$i$-chain} from $u$ to $w$ is a sequence $\beta=(\beta_1,\ldots,\beta_\ell)$ of integers different from $i$ such that, with $u^{(0)}=u$ and $u^{(s)}=u^{(s-1)}t_{i\beta_s}$, every step $u^{(s-1)}\lessdot u^{(s)}$ is a Bruhat cover and $u^{(\ell)}=w$; repetitions among the $\beta_s$ are allowed. Put $c_s(\beta)=u^{(s)}(i)$, the value at position $i$ after $s$ steps, and $\epsilon(\beta)=\prod_s\epsilon_i(\beta_s)$. Every $i$-walk determines the $i$-chain formed by its nontrivial steps, and conversely an $i$-chain of length $\ell\leq k$ arises from exactly the $\binom{k}{\ell}$ walks obtained by choosing the positions $j_1<\cdots<j_p$ ($p=k-\ell$) of the trivial steps. In such a walk the trivial step at $j_r$ is preceded by $j_r-r$ nontrivial steps, so it contributes the factor $x_{j_r}-z_{c_{j_r-r}(\beta)}$. Summing over the choices of $j_1<\cdots<j_p$ and comparing with the explicit formula for $E_{p,k}$ in Definition \ref{definition:pieri}, the walks with chain $\beta$ contribute in total
$$\epsilon(\beta)\sum_{1\leq j_1<\cdots<j_p\leq k}\ \prod_{r=1}^p\big(x_{j_r}-z_{c_{j_r-r}(\beta)}\big)=\epsilon(\beta)\,E_{k-\ell,k}\big(x;z_{c_0(\beta)},z_{c_1(\beta)},\ldots,z_{c_\ell(\beta)}\big).$$
Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chainsum}
\partial^w_u\big(E_{k,k}(x;z_i)\big)=\sum_{\beta}\epsilon(\beta)\,E_{k-\ell(\beta),k}\big(x;z_{c_0(\beta)},\ldots,z_{c_{\ell(\beta)}(\beta)}\big),
\end{equation}
the sum being over all $i$-chains $\beta$ from $u$ to $w$ of length $\ell(\beta)\leq k$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:Ehe} the summand depends on $c_0(\beta),\ldots,c_\ell(\beta)$ only through their multiset.

\emph{Chains without repetitions.} If $\beta_1,\ldots,\beta_\ell$ are distinct, then $\beta$ is a witness for $u\Tom{i}w$, and by Definition \ref{def:itom} it is the unique one; moreover $\ell=\ell(u,w)$, $\epsilon(\beta)=(-1)^{\ell(u,w)-\sigma_i(u,w)}$, and since the position $\beta_s$ is untouched before the $s$-th step, $c_s(\beta)=u(\beta_s)$, so that $\{c_0(\beta),\ldots,c_\ell(\beta)\}=D_i(u,w)$. So the chains without repetitions contribute exactly the right-hand side of the theorem if $u\Tom{i}w$ and $\ell(u,w)\leq k$, and nothing otherwise; if $u\Tom{i}w$ and $\ell(u,w)>k$ then both sides of the theorem vanish, the left by degree. It remains to show that the chains with a repetition contribute zero in total.

\emph{Chains with repetitions.} Let $\beta$ be an $i$-chain from $u$ to $w$ with a repetition, and let $s$ be the least index for which $\beta_s\in\{\beta_1,\ldots,\beta_{s-1}\}$, say $\beta_s=\beta_t$ with $t<s$. Put $m=s-t$ and $(\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_m)=(\beta_t,\ldots,\beta_{s-1})$; these are distinct, and $m\geq2$ because two consecutive covers cannot use the same transposition. The segment $(\beta_t,\ldots,\beta_s)$ of $\beta$ is the sequence $R_1$ of Lemma \ref{lemma:rotation} for $\psi=u^{(t-1)}$, and it is valid. The alternation in Lemma \ref{lemma:rotation}(ii) pairs each valid $R_r$ with $\gamma_r>i$ with the next valid sequence in cyclic order, whose entry lies below $i$, and each valid $R_r$ with $\gamma_r<i$ with the previous one; this is a fixed-point-free involution on the valid sequences which exchanges the two sides of $i$. Let $R_{r^*}$ be the sequence paired with $R_1$, and let $\beta^*$ be obtained from $\beta$ by replacing the segment $(\beta_t,\ldots,\beta_s)$ with $R_{r^*}$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:rotation}(i) the new segment again leads from $u^{(t-1)}$ to $u^{(t-1)}C=u^{(s)}$, so $\beta^*$ is an $i$-chain from $u$ to $w$ of the same length as $\beta$. The entries of $\beta^*$ preceding the index $s$ form the same set as those of $\beta$ and are distinct, and $\beta^*_s=\beta^*_t$, so the first repetition of $\beta^*$ occurs at the same index $s$ with the same $t$, the same $\psi$, and the same cyclic sequence $\gamma$; hence $\beta^{**}=\beta$, and $\beta\mapsto\beta^*$ is a fixed-point-free involution on the set of $i$-chains with repetitions. The values $c_0,\ldots,c_{t-1}$ and $c_s,\ldots,c_\ell$ are the same for $\beta$ and $\beta^*$, since the permutations $u^{(0)},\ldots,u^{(t-1)}$ and $u^{(s)},\ldots,u^{(\ell)}$ are; and $c_t,\ldots,c_{s-1}$ are the values $\psi(\gamma_1),\ldots,\psi(\gamma_m)$ in some order for both chains. So the multisets of values coincide, and the $E$-factors in \eqref{eq:chainsum} agree. Finally $\epsilon(\beta^*)=-\epsilon(\beta)$: the two segments consist of the entries $\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_m$, each once, together with one additional entry, which is $\gamma_1$ for $\beta$ and $\gamma_{r^*}$ for $\beta^*$, and these lie on opposite sides of $i$. Hence the chains with repetitions cancel in pairs in \eqref{eq:chainsum}, and the theorem follows.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Conclusion of the proofs}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem \ref{theorem:orddouble}]
From the definition of the double Schubert polynomials, for every $w$ and $a$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:leftdd}
\partial^a\sch_w(x;z)=\begin{cases}-\sch_{s_aw}(x;z)&\text{if }s_aw<w,\\ 0&\text{if }s_aw>w:\end{cases}
\end{equation}
indeed $\sch_w=(-1)^{\ell(w_0)-\ell(w)}\partial^{ww_0}\sch_{w_0}$, and $\partial^a\partial^{ww_0}$ equals $\partial^{s_aww_0}$ if $\ell(s_aww_0)>\ell(ww_0)$, which is the case $s_aw<w$, and equals $0$ otherwise. Since $\mu=v^{-1}\cdot y$ is reduced, so is $\mu^{-1}=y^{-1}\cdot v$; writing $y=s_{a_1}\cdots s_{a_r}$ reduced, the permutations $s_{a_j}s_{a_{j+1}}\cdots s_{a_r}\mu^{-1}$, $j=r+1,r,\ldots,1$, therefore decrease in length by one at each step, from $\mu^{-1}$ down to $y\mu^{-1}=v$. Applying \eqref{eq:leftdd} $r$ times and then Lemma \ref{lemma:dominantfactor},
$$\sch_v(x;z)=(-1)^{\ell(y)}\,\partial^{y}\sch_{\mu^{-1}}(x;z)=(-1)^{\ell(y)}\,\partial^{y}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^kE_{\theta_i,\theta_i}(x;z_i)\Big).$$
Now expand by the ascending Leibniz formula, Lemma \ref{lemma:skewfacts}(iii) with $u=1$ and $w=y$, and evaluate each factor by Theorem \ref{theorem:skewtop}:
\begin{align*}
\partial^{y}\Big(\prod_{i=1}^kE_{\theta_i,\theta_i}(x;z_i)\Big)&=\sum_{1=v_0\leq\cdots\leq v_k=y}\ \prod_{i=1}^k\partial^{v_i}_{v_{i-1}}\big(E_{\theta_i,\theta_i}(x;z_i)\big)\\
&=\sum_{1=v_0\Tom{1}v_1\Tom{2}\cdots\Tom{k}v_k=y}\ \prod_{i=1}^k(-1)^{\ell(v_{i-1},v_i)-\sigma_i(v_{i-1},v_i)}E_{\theta_i-\ell(v_{i-1},v_i),\,\theta_i}\big(x;z_{D_i(v_{i-1},v_i)}\big).
\end{align*}
Since $\sum_i\ell(v_{i-1},v_i)=\ell(y)$, the signs combine to $\prod_i(-1)^{\sigma_i(v_{i-1},v_i)}$; and $y=v\mu$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem \ref{theorem:quandouble}]
A strict dominant permutation is dominant, so Theorem \ref{theorem:orddouble} applies to $v$ and $\mu$ and expresses $\sch_v(x;z)$ as the sum, over the chains $1=v_0\Tom{1}\cdots\Tom{k}v_k=v\mu$, of $\prod_i(-1)^{\sigma_i}E_{\thop_i-\ell_i,\thop_i}(x;z_{D_i})$, where $\ell_i=\ell(v_{i-1},v_i)$, $\sigma_i=\sigma_i(v_{i-1},v_i)$ and $D_i=D_i(v_{i-1},v_i)$. Apply $\mathcal Q$ to both sides. The left-hand side becomes $\qsch_v(x;z)$. On the right, by Lemma \ref{lemma:Ehe} each factor is $E_{\thop_i-\ell_i,\thop_i}(x;z_{D_i})=\sum_{m}c_{i,m}\,e_m(x_1,\ldots,x_{\thop_i})$ with coefficients $c_{i,m}\in\mathbb Z[z]$, and multiplying out, each term $\prod_ie_{m_i}(x_1,\ldots,x_{\thop_i})$ of the product over $i$ is a standard elementary monomial because $\thop_1>\cdots>\thop_k$. Hence $\mathcal Q$ sends the product to $\prod_i\sum_mc_{i,m}\,e^{(q)}_m(x_1,\ldots,x_{\thop_i})=\prod_i\qelem_{\thop_i-\ell_i,\thop_i}(x;z_{D_i})$, again by Lemma \ref{lemma:Ehe}.
\end{proof}

We prove Theorem \ref{theorem:quandoublemed} by reaching the medium shape from the strict shape by divided differences, one for each retained pair; this also explains the form of the doubled factor. For $k\geq1$ and $i\geq1$ write $\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z_i,z_{i+1})$ for the quantum double Schubert polynomial of the $k\times2$ rectangle (Section \ref{section:additional}) with $(z_1,z_2)$ replaced by $(z_i,z_{i+1})$, i.e.\ $\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z_i,z_{i+1})=\mathcal Q\big(E_{k,k}(x;z_i)E_{k,k}(x;z_{i+1})\big)=\qelem_{k,k;k}(x;z_i,z_{i+1})$.

\begin{corollary}[Factorization for the medium shape] \label{corollary:mediumfactor}
Let $\mu$ be a dominant permutation of medium shape with $\medtheta=\code(\mu)$, paired indices $\mathcal P$ and single indices $\mathcal S$ as in Theorem \ref{theorem:quandoublemed}, and let $\nu=\mu\prod_{i\in\mathcal P}s_i$. Then $\nu$ is strict dominant, $\code(\nu)$ is $\medtheta$ with the first entry of each retained pair increased by one, $\mu=\nu\prod_{i\in\mathcal P}s_i$ with $\ell(\mu)=\ell(\nu)-|\mathcal P|$, and
$$\qsch_{\mu^{-1}}(x;z)=(-1)^{|\mathcal P|}\Big(\prod_{i\in\mathcal P}\partial^{i}\Big)\qsch_{\nu^{-1}}(x;z)=\prod_{i\in\mathcal S}\qelem_{\medtheta_i,\medtheta_i}(x;z_i)\ \prod_{i\in\mathcal P}\qsch_{d_2[\medtheta_i]}(x;z_i,z_{i+1}).$$
In particular, for $\mu=\medperm(v^{-1})$ one has $\nu=\mom(v^{-1})$ by Proposition \ref{prop:weakorder}(iii).
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For $i\in\mathcal P$ with $k=\medtheta_i=\medtheta_{i+1}$ we have $\mu(i)<\mu(i+1)$, so $\ell(\mu s_i)=\ell(\mu)+1$ and $\code(\mu s_i)$ is $\code(\mu)$ with $(\code_i,\code_{i+1})=(k,k)$ replaced by $(k+1,k)$; the indices in $\mathcal P$ are pairwise at distance $\geq2$, so the $s_i$ commute and act on disjoint pairs of positions, which gives the assertions about $\nu$ (its code is strictly decreasing on its nonzero part because the entry preceding a retained pair is $\geq k+2$ and the entry following it is $\leq k-1$). Enumerate $\mathcal P=\{i_1<\cdots<i_r\}$ and put $\mu_0=\mu$, $\mu_t=\mu_{t-1}s_{i_t}$, so $\mu_r=\nu$. Since $s_{i_t}\mu_t^{-1}=(\mu_ts_{i_t})^{-1}=\mu_{t-1}^{-1}$ is shorter than $\mu_t^{-1}$, \eqref{eq:leftdd} gives $\sch_{\mu_{t-1}^{-1}}(x;z)=-\partial^{i_t}\sch_{\mu_t^{-1}}(x;z)$, and iterating, $\sch_{\mu^{-1}}(x;z)=(-1)^r\partial^{i_1}\cdots\partial^{i_r}\sch_{\nu^{-1}}(x;z)$. The map $\mathcal Q$ acts on the $x$-variables and is $\mathbb Z[q][z]$-linear, while the $\partial^a$ act on the $z$-variables and are $\mathbb Z[q][x]$-linear; hence they commute, and applying $\mathcal Q$ gives the first equality. For the second, Lemma \ref{lemma:dominantfactor} gives $\qsch_{\nu^{-1}}(x;z)=\prod_j\qelem_{\code_j(\nu),\code_j(\nu)}(x;z_j)$, in which the factor of index $j\notin\mathcal P\cup(\mathcal P+1)$ is $\qelem_{\medtheta_j,\medtheta_j}(x;z_j)$ and the factors of indices $i,i+1$ for $i\in\mathcal P$, $k=\medtheta_i$, are $\qelem_{k+1,k+1}(x;z_i)\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_{i+1})$. The operator $\partial^i$ involves only $z_i,z_{i+1}$, so it acts on this pair of factors alone, and the operators for distinct $i\in\mathcal P$ act on disjoint sets of variables. Finally, with $P_m(t)=\prod_{j\leq m}(x_j-t)$,
$$P_{k+1}(z_i)P_k(z_{i+1})-P_{k+1}(z_{i+1})P_k(z_i)=P_k(z_i)P_k(z_{i+1})\big[(x_{k+1}-z_i)-(x_{k+1}-z_{i+1})\big]=(z_{i+1}-z_i)\,P_k(z_i)P_k(z_{i+1}),$$
so $-\partial^i\big(P_{k+1}(z_i)P_k(z_{i+1})\big)=P_k(z_i)P_k(z_{i+1})$. Applying $\mathcal Q$, which is multiplicative on $P_{k+1}(z_i)P_k(z_{i+1})$ (distinct alphabets, Lemma \ref{lemma:dominantfactor}) and commutes with $\partial^i$, gives $-\partial^i\big(\qelem_{k+1,k+1}(x;z_i)\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_{i+1})\big)=\mathcal Q\big(P_k(z_i)P_k(z_{i+1})\big)=\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z_i,z_{i+1})$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem \ref{theorem:quandoublemed}]
Exactly as in the proof of Theorem \ref{theorem:orddouble}, $\qsch_v(x;z)=(-1)^{\ell(y)}\partial^y\qsch_{\mu^{-1}}(x;z)$ (the identity \eqref{eq:leftdd} holds for the quantum polynomials, $\mathcal Q$ commuting with the $z$-divided differences). Insert the factorization of Corollary \ref{corollary:mediumfactor} and expand by the ascending Leibniz formula, Lemma \ref{lemma:skewfacts}(iii), treating each doubled factor as a single factor. The skew operators $\partial^w_u$ are $\mathbb Z[q][x]$-linear and so commute with $\mathcal Q$; hence, by Lemma \ref{lemma:skewfacts}(iii) once more and Theorem \ref{theorem:skewtop},
\begin{multline*}
\partial^{w}_{u}\,\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z_i,z_{i+1})=\mathcal Q\Big(\sum_{u\leq u'\leq w}\partial^{u'}_u\big(E_{k,k}(x;z_i)\big)\,\partial^{w}_{u'}\big(E_{k,k}(x;z_{i+1})\big)\Big)\\
=\sum_{u\Tom iu'\Tom{i+1}w}(-1)^{\ell_i+\ell_{i+1}-\sigma_i-\sigma_{i+1}}\,\qelem_{k-\ell_i,\,k-\ell_{i+1};\,k}\big(x;z_{D_i},z_{D_{i+1}}\big),
\end{multline*}
with $\ell_i,\sigma_i,D_i$ attached to $u\Tom iu'$ and $\ell_{i+1},\sigma_{i+1},D_{i+1}$ to $u'\Tom{i+1}w$, while the single factors are evaluated by Theorem \ref{theorem:skewtop} and Lemma \ref{lemma:Ehe} as in the proof of Theorem \ref{theorem:quandouble}. Collecting the signs as in the proof of Theorem \ref{theorem:orddouble} gives the theorem. In this form it is literally Theorem \ref{theorem:quandouble} for $\nu=\mu\prod_{i\in\mathcal P}s_i$ with the divided differences $\partial^i$, $i\in\mathcal P$, applied first: the two Pieri steps at columns $i,i+1$ of the strict shape, in $k+1$ and $k$ variables, are replaced by the doubled step in $k$ variables.
\end{proof}

\section{General-degree and doubled Pieri formulas} \label{section:additional}

In this section we record the two forms of the Pieri rule that the quantum multiplication algorithm of Section \ref{section:algorithms} actually uses: the rule for a quantum factorial elementary polynomial of arbitrary degree $p\leq k$, and a ``doubled'' rule for the quantization of a product of two such polynomials in the \emph{same} $k$ variables. Recall from Definition \ref{definition:pieri} that $n_k(u,w)=k-|\fx_k(u,w)|$ is the number of positions $i\leq k$ at which $u$ and $w$ differ, equivalently the number of transpositions in a chain witnessing $u\tom{k}w$, and that $E_{p,k}(y_A;z)$ denotes the factorial elementary polynomial in the variables $y_a$, $a\in A$ (with $|A|=k$), and $z_1,z_2,\ldots$.

\begin{theorem}[Quantum Pieri formula, general degree] \label{theorem:pierigeneral}
Suppose $u\in S_\infty$ and $0\leq p\leq k$. Then
$$\qsch_u(x;y)\,\qelem_{p,k}(x;z)=\sum_{\substack{u\tom{k} w\\ n_k(u,w)\leq p}}q_k(u,w)\,E_{p-n_k(u,w),\,k-n_k(u,w)}\big(y_{\fx_k(u,w)};z\big)\,\qsch_w(x;y).$$
\end{theorem}

For $p=k$ the coefficient is $q_k(u,w)\wte(y_{\fx_k(u,w)};z)$ and this is Theorem \ref{theorem:pieri}; at $q=0$ it is the Pieri formula of \cite[Theorem~7.1]{samuelmolev}. This is the form implemented in \cite{schubmult} (the routines \texttt{elem\_sym\_perms\_q} and \texttt{elem\_sym\_func\_q}). We deduce the general case from the case $p=k$.

\begin{proof}
Let $X=(X_1,\ldots,X_k)$ be indeterminates. The factorial elementary polynomial
$$E_{p,k}(X;z)=\sum_{1\leq i_1<\cdots<i_p\leq k}\ \prod_{j=1}^p(X_{i_j}-z_{i_j-j+1})$$
is symmetric in $X$ and of degree at most one in each $X_i$, hence is a $\mathbb Z[z]$-linear combination of the elementary symmetric polynomials:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Eexpansion}
E_{p,k}(X;z)=\sum_{j=0}^{p}c_{p,k,j}(z)\,e_j(X_1,\ldots,X_k),\qquad c_{p,k,j}(z)\in\mathbb Z[z].
\end{equation}
Two consequences. First, applying the quantization map $\mathcal Q$ (which is $\mathbb Z[z]$-linear and sends $e_j(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$ to $e^{(q)}_j(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qEexpansion}
\qelem_{p,k}(x;z)=\sum_{j=0}^{p}c_{p,k,j}(z)\,e^{(q)}_j(x_1,\ldots,x_k).
\end{equation}
Second, for $0\leq n\leq k$ extract from \eqref{eq:Eexpansion} the coefficient of the monomial $X_{k-n+1}X_{k-n+2}\cdots X_k$. On the right it is $\sum_jc_{p,k,j}(z)\,e_{j-n}(X_1,\ldots,X_{k-n})$. On the left, a term of $E_{p,k}(X;z)$ contains this monomial exactly when $\{i_{p-n+1},\ldots,i_p\}=\{k-n+1,\ldots,k\}$, and then the remaining factors $\prod_{j\leq p-n}(X_{i_j}-z_{i_j-j+1})$ with $i_1<\cdots<i_{p-n}\leq k-n$ are precisely the terms of $E_{p-n,k-n}(X_1,\ldots,X_{k-n};z)$. Hence, for every set $A$ of $k-n$ indices,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Ereduction}
\sum_{j=0}^{p}c_{p,k,j}(z)\,e_{j-n}(y_A)=E_{p-n,\,k-n}(y_A;z),
\end{equation}
both sides being $0$ when $n>p$.

Now put $z_1=-t$ in Theorem \ref{theorem:pieri}. By Lemma \ref{lemma:qelemkk} the left side becomes $\sum_{j=0}^k t^{k-j}\,\qsch_u(x;y)\,e^{(q)}_j(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$, while on the right $\wte(y_{\fx_k(u,w)};z)=\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,w)}(y_a+t)=\sum_i t^{\,k-n_k(u,w)-i}e_i(y_{\fx_k(u,w)})$, since $|\fx_k(u,w)|=k-n_k(u,w)$. Comparing the coefficients of $t^{k-j}$ gives, for $0\leq j\leq k$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nonfactorialpieri}
\qsch_u(x;y)\,e^{(q)}_j(x_1,\ldots,x_k)=\sum_{u\tom{k}w}q_k(u,w)\,e_{j-n_k(u,w)}\big(y_{\fx_k(u,w)}\big)\,\qsch_w(x;y).
\end{equation}
Multiplying \eqref{eq:nonfactorialpieri} by $c_{p,k,j}(z)$, summing over $j$ and using \eqref{eq:qEexpansion} and then \eqref{eq:Ereduction} with $A=\fx_k(u,w)$ and $n=n_k(u,w)$ yields the theorem.
\end{proof}

\subsection*{The doubled formula}

Recall that the quantization map $\mathcal Q$ of Fomin, Gelfand and Postnikov (Lemma \ref{lemma:quantumtoordinary}) is linear over the coefficient variables, sends each standard elementary monomial $\prod_j e_{i_j}(x_1,\ldots,x_j)$ to $\prod_j e^{(q)}_{i_j}(x_1,\ldots,x_j)$, and satisfies $\mathcal Q(\sch_w(x;z))=\qsch_w(x;z)$. In particular $\mathcal Q(E_{p,k}(x;z))=\qelem_{p,k}(x;z)$, and more generally $\mathcal Q$ is multiplicative on a product of factorial elementary polynomials $E_{p_j,k_j}(x;z^{(j)})$, $j=1,2,\ldots$, whose numbers of variables $k_j$ are \emph{pairwise distinct}, since such a product expands into standard elementary monomials. It is \emph{not} multiplicative when two factors have the same number of variables: in general$$\mathcal Q\big(E_{p_1,k}(x;z)E_{p_2,k}(x;z')\big)\neq\qelem_{p_1,k}(x;z)\,\qelem_{p_2,k}(x;z').$$ The following definition isolates the terms of the iterated Pieri formula \eqref{eq:allk} below which survive in the quantization of the product.

\begin{definition}[Compatible pairs of chains] \label{def:compatible}
Let $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$. We say that the pair of chains is \emph{compatible} if for every nontrivial cycle $d$ of $v^{-1}w$ and every nontrivial cycle $c$ of $u^{-1}v$ with $\max c=\max d$, one has $c\cap d=\{\max d\}$. In words: a cycle of the second step which shares its top with a cycle of the first step may meet that cycle only in the top. (A cycle of $v^{-1}w$ whose top is not the top of any cycle of $u^{-1}v$ is unrestricted.)
\end{definition}

Throughout this subsection we write $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)$ and $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2)$ for the quantum factorial elementary polynomial $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)=\mathcal Q\big(\prod_{i\leq k}(x_i-z_1)\big)$ and for the same polynomial with $z_1$ replaced by $z_2$; thus $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2)$ is $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z')$ for the shifted alphabet $z'=(z_2,z_3,\ldots)$. For a pair of chains $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$ put
$$\omega_k(u,v,w)=q_k(u,v)\,q_k(v,w)\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,v)}(y_a-z_1)\prod_{b\in\fx_k(v,w)}(y_b-z_2),$$
so that two applications of Theorem \ref{theorem:pieri}, the second with $z_1$ replaced by $z_2$, give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:allk}
\qsch_u(x;y)\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2)=\sum_{u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w}\omega_k(u,v,w)\,\qsch_w(x;y).
\end{equation}
Recall that $d_2[k]$ denotes the dominant permutation with $\code(d_2[k])=(2^k)$, whose diagram is a $k\times2$ rectangle, so that
$$\sch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=\prod_{i\leq k}(x_i-z_1)(x_i-z_2)=E_{k,k}(x;z_1)E_{k,k}(x;z_2),\qquad \qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=\mathcal Q\big(E_{k,k}(x;z_1)E_{k,k}(x;z_2)\big);$$
we set $d_2[0]=\mathrm{id}$.

\begin{theorem}[Doubled quantum Pieri formula] \label{theorem:doubledpieri}
For $u\in S_\infty$ and $k\geq1$,
\begin{multline*}
\qsch_u(x;y)\,\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=\sum_{\substack{u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w\\ \text{compatible}}}\omega_k(u,v,w)\,\qsch_w(x;y)\\
=\sum_{\substack{u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w\\ \text{compatible}}}q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w)\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,v)}(y_a-z_1)\prod_{b\in\fx_k(v,w)}(y_b-z_2)\,\qsch_w(x;y).
\end{multline*}
\end{theorem}

Thus the doubled formula is obtained from the iterated formula \eqref{eq:allk} by \emph{omitting} the incompatible pairs, exactly as the quantization $\qsch_{d_2[k]}$ of the product $E_{k,k}(x;z_1)E_{k,k}(x;z_2)$ omits terms from the product $\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2)$ of the quantizations. This is the rule implemented by the routine \texttt{double\_elem\_sym\_q} of \cite{schubmult}, and it is the ``paired step'' of the quantum algorithm of Section \ref{section:algorithms}. We have verified the theorem by computer for all $u\in S_4$ and all $k\leq3$; in these cases the unrestricted sum \eqref{eq:allk} fails to equal the left-hand side in roughly half of the instances, so the compatibility condition is essential.

\begin{example}[Compatible and incompatible pairs] \label{example:doubled}
(a) $k=1$. A step $u\tom1v$ is either trivial or a single cover $t_{1b}$, so a cycle of $u^{-1}v$ and a cycle of $v^{-1}w$ with a common top $B$ sharing the element $1$ are both equal to $(1,B)$, i.e.\ $v=ut_{1B}$ and $w=u$; and $t_{1B}$ can be a cover of both $u$ and $ut_{1B}$ only when $B=2$ (if $B\geq3$, the classical cover among the two requires no intermediate value in the interval between $u(1)$ and $u(B)$, while the quantum cover requires every intermediate value to lie in it). Thus the incompatible pairs are exactly the round trips $u\tom1us_1\tom1u$, in which one step is a classical and the other a quantum cover, both fixed sets are empty, and $\omega_1(u,us_1,u)=q_1$. Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} for $k=1$ therefore says that $\qsch_u\,\qsch_{312}(x;z)$ is obtained from $\qsch_u\,\qelem_{1,1}(x;z_1)\,\qelem_{1,1}(x;z_2)$ by deleting the term $q_1\qsch_u$, in agreement with $\qsch_{312}(x;z)=\mathcal Q\big((x_1-z_1)(x_1-z_2)\big)=(x_1-z_1)(x_1-z_2)-q_1$, which is the case $k=1$ of the recurrence of Lemma \ref{lemma:thetaPP} below. For $u=21$ the six compatible pairs and the rejected round trip $21\tom112\tom121$ are listed in Example \ref{example:algorithm}.

(b) $k=2$, $u=1324$. The steps $u\tom2v$ are: the trivial one; the single covers $t_{13}$ (classical, $v=2314$), $t_{23}$ (quantum, weight $q_2$, $v=1234$) and $t_{24}$ (classical, $v=1423$); and the two-step chains $t_{24}t_{13}$ ($v=2413$) and $t_{13}t_{23}$ (weight $q_2$, $v=2134$). Consider first $w=3124$. Exactly two pairs $u\tom2v\tom2w$ exist, namely
$$1324\xrightarrow{t_{13}}2314\xrightarrow{t_{23}t_{13}}3124\qquad\text{and}\qquad1324\xrightarrow{t_{13}t_{23}}2134\xrightarrow{t_{13}}3124,$$
with $\omega_2=q_2(y_3-z_1)$ and $\omega_2=q_2(y_1-z_2)$ respectively ($\fx_2(1324,2314)=\{3\}$, $\fx_2(2134,3124)=\{1\}$, the other fixed sets being empty). In the first, the cycles $(1,3)$ of $u^{-1}v$ and $(1,2,3)$ of $v^{-1}w$ share the top $3=k+1$ and the element $1$; in the second, the cycles $(2,1,3)$ and $(1,3)$ do. Both pairs are incompatible, so the coefficient of $\qsch_{3124}$ in $\qsch_{1324}\,\qsch_{3412}(x;z)$ is $0$, whereas the coefficient in $\qsch_{1324}\,\qelem_{2,2}(x;z_1)\qelem_{2,2}(x;z_2)$ is $q_2\big[(y_3-z_1)+(y_1-z_2)\big]$. The recurrence of Lemma \ref{lemma:thetaPP} accounts for this difference by $q_2$ times the coefficient of $\qsch_{3124}$ in $\qsch_{1324}\,\qsch_{d_2[1]}(x;z)$, which by (a) is the sum over the two (compatible) level-$1$ pairs $1324\tom11324\tom13124$ and $1324\tom13124\tom13124$, namely $(y_1-z_1)+(y_3-z_2)$; and indeed $(y_3-z_1)+(y_1-z_2)=(y_1-z_1)+(y_3-z_2)$, although the two sums do not agree term by term. For $w=2413$, by contrast, all four pairs
$$1324\tom21324\xrightarrow{t_{24}t_{13}}2413,\quad 1324\xrightarrow{t_{13}}2314\xrightarrow{t_{24}}2413,\quad 1324\xrightarrow{t_{24}}1423\xrightarrow{t_{13}}2413,\quad 1324\xrightarrow{t_{24}t_{13}}2413\tom22413$$
are compatible (the cycles of the two steps have distinct tops, or one step is trivial), and the coefficient of $\qsch_{2413}$ in both products is
$$(y_1-z_1)(y_3-z_1)+(y_3-z_1)(y_2-z_2)+(y_1-z_1)(y_4-z_2)+(y_2-z_2)(y_4-z_2).$$
Both coefficients were checked against the output of \cite{schubmult}.
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}[Doubled formula in lower degrees] \label{corollary:lowerdegrees}
For $u\in S_\infty$, $0\leq p_1,p_2\leq k$ and two alphabets $z,z'$, with $\qelem_{p_1,p_2;k}(x;z,z')=\mathcal Q\big(E_{p_1,k}(x;z)E_{p_2,k}(x;z')\big)$ as in \eqref{eq:qelemdouble}, and writing $n_1=n_k(u,v)$, $n_2=n_k(v,w)$ for a pair $u\tom kv\tom kw$,
\begin{multline*}
\qsch_u(x;y)\,\qelem_{p_1,p_2;k}(x;z,z')\\
=\sum_{\substack{u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w\ \text{compatible}\\ n_1\leq p_1,\ n_2\leq p_2}}q_k(u,v)\,q_k(v,w)\,E_{p_1-n_1,\,k-n_1}\big(y_{\fx_k(u,v)};z\big)\,E_{p_2-n_2,\,k-n_2}\big(y_{\fx_k(v,w)};z'\big)\,\qsch_w(x;y).
\end{multline*} For $p_1=p_2=k$ this is Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri}. This is the form implemented in the routine \texttt{double\_elem\_sym\_q} of \cite{schubmult} and used by the paired step of Method \ref{method:schubmultq}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} is an identity of polynomials in $z_1,z_2$, in which $\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=\mathcal Q\big(\prod_{i\leq k}(x_i-z_1)\prod_{i\leq k}(x_i-z_2)\big)$ and $\mathcal Q$ is linear over $\mathbb Z[z_1,z_2]$. Substitute $z_1=-t$, $z_2=-t'$ for independent indeterminates $t,t'$. On the left, $\prod_{i\leq k}(x_i+t)\prod_{i\leq k}(x_i+t')=\sum_{a,b=0}^kt^{k-a}t'^{\,k-b}e_a(x_1,\ldots,x_k)e_b(x_1,\ldots,x_k)$. On the right, for a pair $u\tom kv\tom kw$ with $n_1=n_k(u,v)$ and $n_2=n_k(v,w)$ we have $\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,v)}(y_a+t)=\sum_it^{k-n_1-i}e_i(y_{\fx_k(u,v)})$ and similarly for the second step, since $|\fx_k(u,v)|=k-n_1$. Comparing the coefficients of $t^{k-a}t'^{\,k-b}$ gives, for $0\leq a,b\leq k$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nonfactorialdoubled}
\qsch_u(x;y)\,\mathcal Q\big(e_a(x_{\leq k})e_b(x_{\leq k})\big)=\sum_{\substack{u\tom kv\tom kw\\\text{compatible}}}q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w)\,e_{a-n_k(u,v)}\big(y_{\fx_k(u,v)}\big)\,e_{b-n_k(v,w)}\big(y_{\fx_k(v,w)}\big)\,\qsch_w(x;y).
\end{equation}
Now expand both factors by \eqref{eq:Eexpansion}: $E_{p_1,k}(x;z)E_{p_2,k}(x;z')=\sum_{a,b}c_{p_1,k,a}(z)c_{p_2,k,b}(z')\,e_a(x_{\leq k})e_b(x_{\leq k})$. Apply $\mathcal Q$, which is linear over $\mathbb Z[z,z']$, multiply by $\qsch_u(x;y)$, insert \eqref{eq:nonfactorialdoubled}, and sum first over $a$ and then over $b$ using \eqref{eq:Ereduction} with $(A,n)=(\fx_k(u,v),n_k(u,v))$ and $(\fx_k(v,w),n_k(v,w))$ respectively; this gives the asserted coefficient, which vanishes unless $n_k(u,v)\leq p_1$ and $n_k(v,w)\leq p_2$.
\end{proof}

We first reduce Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} to a single combinatorial identity about pairs of chains, \eqref{eq:Jk} below (Proposition \ref{prop:doubledreduction}). To prove \eqref{eq:Jk} we pass to a termwise reformulation \eqref{eq:Bk} based on the quantum Giambelli formula, prove that the pairs of chains organize into blocks along which \eqref{eq:Bk} telescopes (Lemma \ref{lemma:blocks}), reduce \eqref{eq:Bk} to an identity between the ends of the blocks and the mixed chains that move the position $k+1$ (Proposition \ref{prop:junction}), and prove that identity by an explicit injection, built from a single local lemma which generalizes the up/down pairing of Lemma \ref{lemma:updown} (Theorem \ref{theorem:junction}). This completes the proof of Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} for all $k$.

\subsubsection*{The recurrence}

The quantization of two equal columns is governed by the following recurrence, in which only powers of $q$ appear.

\begin{lemma}[Quantization of two equal columns] \label{lemma:thetaPP}
For all $k\geq1$,
$$\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2)=\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)+q_k\,\qsch_{d_2[k-1]}(x;z).$$
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $P_k(z_1)=\prod_{i\leq k}(x_i-z_1)=E_{k,k}(x;z)$ and $Q_k(z_1)=\mathcal Q(P_k(z_1))=\qelem_{k,k}(x;z)$, and similarly with $z_2$, with $P_0=Q_0=1$ and $P_{-1}=Q_{-1}=0$; thus $\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)$ equals $\mathcal Q(P_k(z_1)P_k(z_2))$. Since $P_{k+1}(z_2)=(x_{k+1}-z_2)P_k(z_2)$, we have
$$P_k(z_1)P_{k+1}(z_2)-P_{k+1}(z_1)P_k(z_2)=(z_1-z_2)\,P_k(z_1)P_k(z_2).$$
The products $P_k(z_1)P_{k+1}(z_2)$ and $P_{k+1}(z_1)P_k(z_2)$ are $\mathbb Z[z_1,z_2]$-linear combinations of the standard elementary monomials $e_a(x_{\leq k})e_b(x_{\leq k+1})$, so $\mathcal Q$ acts on them multiplicatively, and
$$(z_1-z_2)\,\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=W_k,\qquad W_k:=Q_k(z_1)Q_{k+1}(z_2)-Q_{k+1}(z_1)Q_k(z_2).$$
The three-term recursion $Q_{k+1}(z_2)=(x_{k+1}-z_2)Q_k(z_2)+q_kQ_{k-1}(z_2)$ of Lemma \ref{lemma:elemrecursion} gives
\begin{multline*}
W_k=Q_k(z_1)\big[(x_{k+1}-z_2)Q_k(z_2)+q_kQ_{k-1}(z_2)\big]-\big[(x_{k+1}-z_1)Q_k(z_1)+q_kQ_{k-1}(z_1)\big]Q_k(z_2)\\
=(z_1-z_2)Q_k(z_1)Q_k(z_2)-q_kW_{k-1}.
\end{multline*}
Dividing by $z_1-z_2$ (all terms are divisible, $W_k$ being antisymmetric in $z_1,z_2$) gives $\qsch_{d_2[k]}=Q_kQ_k-q_k\qsch_{d_2[k-1]}$.
\end{proof}

Lemma \ref{lemma:thetaPP} reduces Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} to a recurrence in $k$. For $u,w\in S_\infty$ let $\mathrm{All}_k(u,w)$, $\mathrm{Comp}_k(u,w)$ and $\mathrm{Inc}_k(u,w)$ denote the sum of $\omega_k(u,v,w)$ over all pairs $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$, over the compatible pairs, and over the incompatible pairs, respectively, so that $\mathrm{All}_k=\mathrm{Comp}_k+\mathrm{Inc}_k$ and, by \eqref{eq:allk}, $\mathrm{All}_k(u,w)=[\qsch_w]\big(\qsch_u\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2)\big)$; here $\mathrm{Comp}_0(u,w)=\mathrm{All}_0(u,w)=\delta_{u,w}$.

\begin{proposition}[Reduction] \label{prop:doubledreduction}
Suppose Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} holds with $k$ replaced by $k-1$ (for $k=1$ this is the trivial statement $\qsch_u\qsch_{d_2[0]}=\qsch_u$). Then Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} holds for $k$ if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Jk}
\mathrm{Inc}_k(u,w)=q_k\,\mathrm{Comp}_{k-1}(u,w)\qquad\text{for all }u,w\in S_\infty.\tag{$\mathrm J_k$}
\end{equation}
Consequently Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} holds for all $k\leq K$ if and only if \eqref{eq:Jk} holds for all $k\leq K$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By Lemma \ref{lemma:thetaPP}, \eqref{eq:allk} and the hypothesis,
$$[\qsch_w]\big(\qsch_u\,\qsch_{d_2[k]}\big)=\mathrm{All}_k(u,w)-q_k[\qsch_w]\big(\qsch_u\,\qsch_{d_2[k-1]}\big)=\mathrm{All}_k(u,w)-q_k\,\mathrm{Comp}_{k-1}(u,w),$$
while $\mathrm{Comp}_k=\mathrm{All}_k-\mathrm{Inc}_k$. The two sides of Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} therefore differ by $\mathrm{Inc}_k(u,w)-q_k\mathrm{Comp}_{k-1}(u,w)$. The last assertion follows by induction on $k$.
\end{proof}

The identity \eqref{eq:Jk} says that the terms omitted from the iterated Pieri formula at level $k$ are exactly accounted for, with an extra factor $q_k$, by the doubled formula one level down. Its left-hand side is a sum over pairs of chains whose cycles overlap, and the following lemma locates the overlap.

\begin{lemma}[Overlapping cycles have top $k+1$] \label{lemma:overlap}
Let $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$, and suppose that a cycle $c$ of $u^{-1}v$ and a cycle $d$ of $v^{-1}w$ have the same top $B$ and share an element $a\leq k$. Then $B=k+1$, and moreover $k\in c\cup d$, i.e.\ one of the two cycles contains the transposition $t_{k,k+1}$. Consequently a pair of chains in which either step contains no transposition $t_{\cdot,k+1}$ is compatible, and in an incompatible pair both steps contain a cycle with top $k+1$, these two cycles overlap, no other pair of cycles does, and the letter $t_{k,k+1}$ occurs in one of the two chains.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
We show more generally that there is no position $r\notin c\cup d$ with $a'<r<B$ for every $a'\in(c\cup d)\setminus\{B\}$; applied to $r=k+1$ this gives $B=k+1$ (as $k+1\notin c\cup d$ when $B\geq k+2$), and applied to $r=k$ it gives $k\in c\cup d$. Suppose such an $r$ exists. By Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(iv) and Lemma \ref{lemma:piericycle} the fixed sets and weights of the two chains do not depend on the order in which their cycles are processed, and every order gives a chain of covers; we process $c$ last in the first step and $d$ first in the second. Write $u'$ for the permutation obtained from $u$ by applying the other cycles of $u^{-1}v$, so that $v=u'c$ and $c$ is $u'$-tight, and write $c=(a_p,\ldots,a_1,B)$, $\beta_0=u'(B)=u(B)$, $\beta_l=u'(a_l)=u(a_l)$, and $d=(b_r,\ldots,b_1,B)$, $\gamma_0=v(B)$, $\gamma_m=v(b_m)$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(i), $v(B)=\beta_p$ and $v(a_l)=\beta_{l-1}$ for $1\leq l\leq p$; as $a=a_i=b_j$ with $i,j\geq1$, we get
$$\gamma_0=\beta_p,\qquad \gamma_j=\beta_{i-1},\qquad\text{and}\qquad \beta_{i-1}\neq\beta_p.$$
Let $\eta$ be the value at the position $r$ when the letters of $c$ are applied. Since $r\notin c\cup d$ and nothing happens between the last letter of $c$ and the first letter of $d$, $\eta$ is also the value at position $r$ when the letters of $d$ are applied. Moreover $a_l<r<B$ for every $l$ and $b_m<r<B$ for every $m$, so the conditions \eqref{eq:Cj} and \eqref{eq:Qj} apply to the position $r$ at every step of $c$ and of $d$; for $c$ they read: $\eta\notin(\beta_l,\beta_{l-1})$ if $\beta_l<\beta_{l-1}$, and $\eta\in(\beta_{l-1},\beta_l)$ if $\beta_l>\beta_{l-1}$, for $1\leq l\leq p$. By Corollary \ref{corollary:cyclic} the cyclic sequence $\Gamma=(\beta_0,\ldots,\beta_p)$ is unimodal, and the displayed conditions say precisely that $\eta$ lies in the gap $(\beta_p,\beta_0)$ of $\Gamma$: if $\Gamma$ has no descent then $\eta\notin(\beta_p,\beta_0)$, i.e.\ $\eta<\beta_p=\min\Gamma$ or $\eta>\beta_0=\max\Gamma$; and if the ascent of $\Gamma$ is $(\beta_{q-1},\beta_q)$ then $\eta\in(\min\Gamma,\max\Gamma)$ and $\eta$ avoids all the descent gaps $(\beta_l,\beta_{l-1})$ with $l\neq q$, which together with $(\beta_0,\beta_p)$ cover $(\min\Gamma,\max\Gamma)\setminus\Gamma$, so $\beta_0<\eta<\beta_p$. In other words, in the cyclically decreasing arrangement of $\Gamma\cup\{\eta\}$ the value $\eta$ sits between $\beta_p$ and $\beta_0$. The same argument for $d$ shows that in the cyclically decreasing arrangement of $\Gamma'\cup\{\eta\}$, $\Gamma'=(\gamma_0,\ldots,\gamma_r)$, the value $\eta$ sits between $\gamma_r$ and $\gamma_0=\beta_p$.

If $\eta>\beta_p$, the first statement forces $\beta_p=\min\Gamma$ and $\eta>\max\Gamma$, while the second forces $\Gamma'\cap(\beta_p,\eta)=\emptyset$; but $\beta_{i-1}\in\Gamma\setminus\{\beta_p\}$ lies in $(\beta_p,\eta)$ and belongs to $\Gamma'$, a contradiction. If $\eta<\beta_p$, the second statement forces $\eta=\min(\Gamma'\cup\{\eta\})$ and $\beta_p=\max\Gamma'$, while the first forces $\Gamma\cap(\eta,\beta_p)=\emptyset$; but $\beta_{i-1}\in\Gamma'\setminus\{\beta_p\}$ lies in $(\eta,\beta_p)$ and belongs to $\Gamma$, again a contradiction. This proves the claim, hence $B=k+1$ and $k\in c\cup d$. The consequences follow because a step contains at most one cycle with a given top.
\end{proof}

The identity \eqref{eq:Jk} will be established for all $k$ in Theorem \ref{theorem:junction}. Before that we record two features of it which any proof must account for, and which explain the detour through \eqref{eq:Bk}. First, by Lemma \ref{lemma:overlap}, in every incompatible pair the overlapping cycles have top $B=k+1$ and one of them contains $k$, so that both steps contain a transposition $t_{a,k+1}$ and one of them contains $t_{k,k+1}$; the incompatibility is a ``detour'' of the level-$(k-1)$ transposition $t_{ak}$ through the position $k+1$, in accordance with $t_{a,k+1}t_{k,k+1}t_{a,k+1}=t_{ak}$. Second, \eqref{eq:Jk} is not a termwise identity: already for $u=1324$, $k=2$, $w=3124$ (Example \ref{example:doubled}(b)) the left-hand side is $q_2\big[(y_3-z_1)+(y_1-z_2)\big]$ and the right-hand side is $q_2\big[(y_1-z_1)+(y_3-z_2)\big]$. In all examples the pairs with given $(u,w)$ and $q$-weight organize into chains $v_1,\ldots,v_{m+1}$ whose fixed sets are nested, with contributions $\prod_{j<i}(y_{c_j}-z_1)\prod_{j>i}(y_{c_j}-z_2)$, which sum to the divided difference $\big(\prod_j(y_{c_j}-z_1)-\prod_j(y_{c_j}-z_2)\big)/(z_2-z_1)$ and so depend only on the set $\{c_j\}$; the identity \eqref{eq:Jk} then amounts to a matching of these sets between the two levels. We now explain the origin of this divided-difference structure and use it to recast \eqref{eq:Jk} as a termwise identity, which we then prove.

\subsubsection*{A termwise reformulation via the Giambelli formula}

The proof of Lemma \ref{lemma:thetaPP} shows that
$$(z_1-z_2)\,\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)=\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)\,\qelem_{k+1,k+1}(x;z_2)-\qelem_{k+1,k+1}(x;z_1)\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2),$$
which is the quantum Giambelli formula \cite{fomin1997quantum} for the $k\times2$ rectangle written as a divided difference. Expanding both products on the right by Theorem \ref{theorem:pieri} at the two levels $k$ and $k+1$ gives the following reformulation. For $u,w\in S_\infty$ put
\begin{align*}
M_k(u,w)&=\sum_{u\tom{k}v\tom{k+1}w}q_k(u,v)\,q_{k+1}(v,w)\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,v)}(y_a-z_1)\prod_{b\in\fx_{k+1}(v,w)}(y_b-z_2),\\
M'_k(u,w)&=\sum_{u\tom{k+1}v\tom{k}w}q_{k+1}(u,v)\,q_k(v,w)\prod_{a\in\fx_{k+1}(u,v)}(y_a-z_1)\prod_{b\in\fx_k(v,w)}(y_b-z_2).
\end{align*}

\begin{proposition}[Giambelli form] \label{prop:giambelliform}
For every $k\geq1$ and all $u,w\in S_\infty$,
$$(z_1-z_2)\,[\qsch_w]\big(\qsch_u(x;y)\,\qsch_{d_2[k]}(x;z)\big)=M_k(u,w)-M'_k(u,w).$$
Consequently Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} holds for $k$ if and only if $(z_1-z_2)\,\mathrm{Comp}_k(u,w)=M_k(u,w)-M'_k(u,w)$ for all $u,w$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Multiply the displayed Giambelli identity by $\qsch_u(x;y)$ and expand $\qsch_u\,\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_1)\qelem_{k+1,k+1}(x;z_2)$ by two applications of Theorem \ref{theorem:pieri}, first at level $k$ with the variable $z_1$ and then at level $k+1$ with the variable $z_2$; this gives $M_k$. The product $\qsch_u\,\qelem_{k+1,k+1}(x;z_1)\qelem_{k,k}(x;z_2)$, expanded first at level $k+1$ and then at level $k$, gives $M'_k$.
\end{proof}

The right-hand side of Proposition \ref{prop:giambelliform} is a sum of products in which the levels of the two steps differ, and the factor $z_1-z_2$ on the left can be absorbed into the compatible pairs as follows. For a pair $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$ let $c(v)=v(k+1)$. If the first step does not use the position $k+1$ then $c(v)=u(k+1)$, and otherwise $c(v)=u(a)$ for a position $a\leq k$ moved by the first step; in either case $c(v)\notin\fx_k(u,v)$, and $c(v)\notin\fx_k(v,w)\subseteq\{v(1),\ldots,v(k)\}$. Hence, writing $z_1-z_2=(y_{c(v)}-z_2)-(y_{c(v)}-z_1)$,
\begin{multline*}
(z_1-z_2)\,\omega_k(u,v,w)\\
=q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w)\Big[\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,v)}(y_a-z_1)\prod_{b\in\fx_k(v,w)\cup\{c(v)\}}(y_b-z_2)-\prod_{a\in\fx_k(u,v)\cup\{c(v)\}}(y_a-z_1)\prod_{b\in\fx_k(v,w)}(y_b-z_2)\Big],
\end{multline*}
and each of the two products on the right has the same shape as a term of $M_k$ or $M'_k$. Writing $\mathcal T(u,w)$ for the multiset of triples $\big(q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w),\ \fx_k(u,v),\ \fx_k(v,w)\cup\{c(v)\}\big)$ over the compatible pairs $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$, $\mathcal S(u,w)$ for the multiset of $\big(q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w),\ \fx_k(u,v)\cup\{c(v)\},\ \fx_k(v,w)\big)$ over the same pairs, $\mathcal M_k(u,w)$ for the multiset of $\big(q_k(u,v)q_{k+1}(v,w),\fx_k(u,v),\fx_{k+1}(v,w)\big)$ over the chains $u\tom{k}v\tom{k+1}w$ and $\mathcal M'_k(u,w)$ for the corresponding multiset over the chains $u\tom{k+1}v\tom{k}w$, we are led to the following termwise statement, from which Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} follows immediately by Proposition \ref{prop:giambelliform}.

\begin{theorem}[Termwise form] \label{theorem:termwise}
For all $k\geq1$ and $u,w\in S_\infty$ there is an equality of multisets
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Bk}
\mathcal T(u,w)\uplus\mathcal M'_k(u,w)=\mathcal S(u,w)\uplus\mathcal M_k(u,w).\tag{$\mathrm B_k$}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Proposition \ref{prop:junction} and Theorem \ref{theorem:junction} below.
\end{proof}

Unlike \eqref{eq:Jk}, the identity \eqref{eq:Bk} is an equality of multisets, so it calls for a bijection. We first show that the greater part of this bijection is forced: the compatible pairs with given $(u,w)$ fall into explicit \emph{blocks}, inside which the terms of $\mathcal T$ and $\mathcal S$ cancel telescopically, and the unblocked ends of the blocks are matched with the chains of $\mathcal M_k$ and $\mathcal M'_k$ that do not move the position $k+1$. This reduces \eqref{eq:Bk} to an identity between the blocked ends of the blocks and the mixed chains that do move $k+1$ (Proposition \ref{prop:junction}); in the simplest situation that identity is exactly the up/down pairing of Lemma \ref{lemma:updown}, and we then prove it in general (Theorem \ref{theorem:junction}).

\subsubsection*{Blocks, and the reduction of \eqref{eq:Bk} to its junctions}

For a pair $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$ we write $[A\mid B]=\prod_{a\in A}(y_a-z_1)\prod_{b\in B}(y_b-z_2)$ and
$$T(v)=q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w)\,[\fx_k(u,v)\mid\fx_k(v,w)\cup\{c(v)\}],\qquad S(v)=q_k(u,v)q_k(v,w)\,[\fx_k(u,v)\cup\{c(v)\}\mid\fx_k(v,w)],$$
so that $(z_1-z_2)\,\omega_k(u,v,w)=T(v)-S(v)$ by the display above, and $\mathcal T(u,w)$, $\mathcal S(u,w)$ are the multisets of the $T(v)$, $S(v)$ over the compatible pairs (we identify the monomial $q[A\mid B]$ with the triple $(q,A,B)$). Recall from Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(iv) and Lemma \ref{lemma:piericycle} that the cycles of a step may be processed in any order.

\begin{definition}[Block data, cut, block] \label{def:blockdata}
Let $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$ be compatible, $\pi_1=u^{-1}v$, $\pi_2=v^{-1}w$. Let $c$ be the cycle of $\pi_1$ with top $k+1$ and $d$ the cycle of $\pi_2$ with top $k+1$ (either may be trivial). By compatibility $c\cap d\subseteq\{k+1\}$, so writing $c=(x_i,\ldots,x_1,k+1)$ and $d=(x_n,\ldots,x_{i+1},k+1)$ we have, by \eqref{eq:pivot},
$$X:=cd=t_{x_1,k+1}\cdots t_{x_n,k+1}=(x_n,\ldots,x_1,k+1),$$
a cycle with top $k+1$ which we call the \emph{merged cycle} of the pair. Let $\rho_1=\pi_1c^{-1}$ and $\rho_2=d^{-1}\pi_2$ be the products of the remaining cycles of $\pi_1$ and of $\pi_2$; all of these have tops $\geq k+2$, and $u^{-1}w=\rho_1X\rho_2$. We call $(\rho_1,X,\rho_2)$ the \emph{block data} and $i$ the \emph{cut} of the pair, and we put
$$i_{\min}=\max\big(\{j:x_j\in\supp\rho_2\}\cup\{0\}\big),\qquad i_{\max}=\min\big(\{j:x_j\in\supp\rho_1\}\cup\{n+1\}\big)-1.$$
Since the cycles of $\pi_1$ are disjoint, $x_1,\ldots,x_i\notin\supp\rho_1$, and likewise $x_{i+1},\ldots,x_n\notin\supp\rho_2$; hence $i_{\min}\leq i\leq i_{\max}$. The \emph{block} of the pair is the set of compatible pairs $u\tom{k}v'\tom{k}w$ with the same block data; the blocks partition the compatible pairs with given $(u,w)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Blocks] \label{lemma:blocks}
Let $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$ be compatible with block data $(\rho_1,X,\rho_2)$, $X=(x_n,\ldots,x_1,k+1)$, and put $u'=u\rho_1$, $c_j=(x_j,\ldots,x_1,k+1)$, $d_j=(x_n,\ldots,x_{j+1},k+1)$ and $v_j=u'c_j$ for $0\leq j\leq n$, so that $v=v_i$ where $i$ is the cut. Then the block of the pair is $\{v_j: i_{\min}\leq j\leq i_{\max}\}$, and for every $j$ in this range:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $u\tom{k}v_j\tom{k}w$ is a compatible pair with block data $(\rho_1,X,\rho_2)$ and cut $j$, and $q_k(u,v_j)\,q_k(v_j,w)=q_k(u,v)\,q_k(v,w)$;
\item[(ii)] $\fx_k(u,v_j)=F_1\setminus\{u(x_1),\ldots,u(x_j)\}$ and $\fx_k(v_j,w)=F_2\setminus\{w(x_{j+1}),\ldots,w(x_n)\}$, where $F_1=\{u(a):a\leq k,\ a\notin\supp\rho_1\}$ and $F_2=\{w(a):a\leq k,\ a\notin\supp\rho_2\}$;
\item[(iii)] $c(v_j)=u(x_j)$ if $j\geq1$ and $c(v_0)=u(k+1)$; moreover $w(x_j)=c(v_{j-1})$ for $i_{\min}<j\leq i_{\max}$.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently $S(v_j)=T(v_{j-1})$ for $i_{\min}<j\leq i_{\max}$, and the contribution of the block telescopes:
$$(z_1-z_2)\sum_{j=i_{\min}}^{i_{\max}}\omega_k(u,v_j,w)=T(v_{i_{\max}})-S(v_{i_{\min}}).$$
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
(i) For $j\leq i_{\max}$ the positions $x_1,\ldots,x_j$ avoid $\supp\rho_1$, so $\rho_1c_j$ is a product of disjoint cycles, each with exactly one element greater than $k$; similarly $d_j\rho_2$ for $j\geq i_{\min}$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:piericycle} and Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(iv), $u\tom{k}v_j\tom{k}w$ holds if and only if $\rho_1$ is $u$-tight, $c_j$ is $u'$-tight, $d_j$ is $v_j$-tight, and $\rho_2$ is $v_jd_j$-tight, where $v_jd_j=u'X$ does not depend on $j$. The first and the last condition do not involve $j$, and they hold because they hold for $j=i$ (as $\pi_1=\rho_1c$ is $u$-tight and $\pi_2=d\rho_2$ is $v$-tight). For the two middle conditions write $\beta_0=u'(k+1)$ and $\beta_l=u'(x_l)$ for $1\leq l\leq n$. The pivot factorization of $X$ is the concatenation of those of $c_j$ and $d_j$, so by Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(i) applied to $X$ and $u'$, the $l$-th letter $t_{x_l,k+1}$ exchanges the value $\beta_l$ at $x_l$ with the value $\beta_{l-1}$ at $k+1$, for every $l$ and independently of $j$; in particular $v_j(k+1)=\beta_j$ and $v_j(x_l)=\beta_l$ for $l>j$. Hence
$$\desc_{u'}(c_j)=\{x_l:l\leq j,\ \beta_l>\beta_{l-1}\},\qquad \desc_{v_j}(d_j)=\{x_l:l>j,\ \beta_l>\beta_{l-1}\},$$
and by Definition \ref{def:tight}
$$\Lw_{u'}(c_j)+\Lw_{v_j}(d_j)=n-2\sum_{l:\,\beta_l>\beta_{l-1}}(k+1-x_l)=\Lw_{u'}(X),\qquad \qw_{u'}(c_j)\,\qw_{v_j}(d_j)=\prod_{l:\,\beta_l>\beta_{l-1}}q_{x_l,k+1}=\qw_{u'}(X),$$
both independent of $j$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(ii),
$$\ell(u'X)-\ell(u')=\big(\ell(v_j)-\ell(u')\big)+\big(\ell(u'X)-\ell(v_j)\big)\geq\Lw_{u'}(c_j)+\Lw_{v_j}(d_j)=\Lw_{u'}(X),$$
with equality if and only if $c_j$ is $u'$-tight and $d_j$ is $v_j$-tight. Equality holds for $j=i$, hence for every $j$. By Corollary \ref{corollary:cycleweight} and Lemma \ref{lemma:tight}(iv), $q_k(u,v_j)q_k(v_j,w)=\qw_u(\rho_1)\,\qw_{u'}(c_j)\,\qw_{v_j}(d_j)\,\qw_{u'X}(\rho_2)=\qw_u(\rho_1)\qw_{u'}(X)\qw_{u'X}(\rho_2)$, independent of $j$. The pair is compatible, since its cycles with tops $\geq k+2$ are those of $\rho_1$ and $\rho_2$, as for the given pair, and $c_j\cap d_j=\{k+1\}$; its block data is $(\rho_1,X,\rho_2)$ and its cut is $j$ by construction. Conversely every pair in the block has cut in $[i_{\min},i_{\max}]$ and is therefore one of the $v_j$, since the block data and the cut determine $\pi_1=\rho_1c_j$.

(ii) The positions $a\leq k$ moved by $\pi_1^{(j)}=\rho_1c_j$ are those of $\supp\rho_1$ together with $x_1,\ldots,x_j$, which gives the first formula (note $u(x_l)\in F_1$ for $l\leq i_{\max}$). A position $a\leq k$ is fixed by $d_j\rho_2$ if and only if $a\notin\supp\rho_2\cup\{x_{j+1},\ldots,x_n\}$, and then $v_j(a)=w(a)$; this gives the second formula (note $w(x_l)\in F_2$ for $l>i_{\min}$).

(iii) We saw that $v_j(k+1)=\beta_j$, which is $u'(x_j)=u(x_j)$ for $1\leq j\leq i_{\max}$ (as $x_j\notin\supp\rho_1$) and $u'(k+1)=u(k+1)$ for $j=0$ (as $k+1\notin\supp\rho_1$). Now let $i_{\min}<j\leq i_{\max}$ and consider the pair $v_{j-1}$. The position $x_j$ is not moved by its first step $\rho_1c_{j-1}$. Its second step is $d_{j-1}\rho_2$, whose first letter $t_{x_j,k+1}$ moves the value $v_{j-1}(k+1)=c(v_{j-1})$ to the position $x_j$, and $x_j$ is moved by no later letter: not by the remaining letters of $d_{j-1}$, and not by $\rho_2$ because $j>i_{\min}$. Hence $w(x_j)=c(v_{j-1})$.

For the consequence write $A_j=\fx_k(u,v_j)$ and $B_j=\fx_k(v_j,w)$. For $i_{\min}<j\leq i_{\max}$, (ii) and (iii) give $A_j\cup\{c(v_j)\}=A_j\cup\{u(x_j)\}=A_{j-1}$ and $B_j=B_{j-1}\cup\{w(x_j)\}=B_{j-1}\cup\{c(v_{j-1})\}$, while the $q$-weights agree by (i); thus $S(v_j)=T(v_{j-1})$. Summing $(z_1-z_2)\omega_k(u,v_j,w)=T(v_j)-S(v_j)$ over the block, all terms cancel except $T(v_{i_{\max}})$ and $S(v_{i_{\min}})$.
\end{proof}

We call $v_{i_{\max}}$ the \emph{head} and $v_{i_{\min}}$ the \emph{tail} of the block; the head is \emph{blocked} if $i_{\max}<n$ (some $x_j$ lies in $\supp\rho_1$) and the tail is \emph{blocked} if $i_{\min}>0$ (some $x_j$ lies in $\supp\rho_2$). Thus a block is the set of all cuts of the merged transposition word $t_{x_1,k+1}\cdots t_{x_n,k+1}$ that are not obstructed by a spectator cycle of $\rho_1$ or $\rho_2$ sharing a position with $X$, and the transit value $c(v_j)$ runs through the values of $u$ at $x_n,x_{n-1},\ldots,x_1,k+1$ as the cut moves from right to left.

\begin{proposition}[Reduction of \eqref{eq:Bk} to junctions] \label{prop:junction}
Let $u,w\in S_\infty$. Let $\mathcal M_k^{\mathrm{mov}}(u,w)\subseteq\mathcal M_k(u,w)$ be the sub-multiset of the chains $u\tom{k}v\tom{k+1}w$ whose second step moves the position $k+1$, let $\mathcal M'^{\,\mathrm{mov}}_k(u,w)\subseteq\mathcal M'_k(u,w)$ consist of the chains $u\tom{k+1}v\tom{k}w$ whose first step moves $k+1$, and let $\mathcal T^{\mathrm{bl}}(u,w)$, respectively $\mathcal S^{\mathrm{bl}}(u,w)$, be the multiset of $T(v)$ over the blocked heads $v$, respectively of $S(v)$ over the blocked tails $v$, of the blocks of compatible pairs $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$. Then \eqref{eq:Bk} holds for $(u,w)$ if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:junction}
\mathcal T^{\mathrm{bl}}(u,w)\uplus\mathcal M'^{\,\mathrm{mov}}_k(u,w)=\mathcal S^{\mathrm{bl}}(u,w)\uplus\mathcal M^{\mathrm{mov}}_k(u,w),\tag{$\mathrm J^{\mathrm B}_k$}
\end{equation}
and in that case $(z_1-z_2)\,\mathrm{Comp}_k(u,w)=M_k(u,w)-M'_k(u,w)$. In particular, Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri} holds for $k$ as soon as \eqref{eq:junction} holds for all $u,w\in S_\infty$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By Lemma \ref{lemma:blocks} the compatible pairs with given $(u,w)$ are partitioned into blocks, and inside each block the elements $S(v_j)$, $i_{\min}<j\leq i_{\max}$, of $\mathcal S(u,w)$ coincide with the elements $T(v_{j-1})$ of $\mathcal T(u,w)$. Cancelling them, \eqref{eq:Bk} is equivalent to
$$\{T(v):v\text{ a head}\}\uplus\mathcal M'_k(u,w)=\{S(v):v\text{ a tail}\}\uplus\mathcal M_k(u,w).$$
Now let $v=v_n$ be an unblocked head, so $d=1$ and $v(k+1)=w(k+1)$. By Lemma \ref{lemma:leveldichotomy}(i), applied with $k+1$ in place of $k$, $v\tom{k+1}w$ with $q_{k+1}(v,w)=q_k(v,w)$ and $\fx_{k+1}(v,w)=\fx_k(v,w)\cup\{v(k+1)\}$; thus $u\tom{k}v\tom{k+1}w$ is a chain of $\mathcal M_k(u,w)\setminus\mathcal M_k^{\mathrm{mov}}(u,w)$, and its triple is $T(v)$. Conversely, let $u\tom{k}v\tom{k+1}w$ be a chain not moving $k+1$. By the same lemma $v\tom{k}w$ with the same weight, the second step has no cycle with top $k+1$, so the pair $u\tom{k}v\tom{k}w$ is compatible with cut $i=n$; hence it is the head of its block, this head is unblocked ($x_1,\ldots,x_n$ avoid $\supp\rho_1$ as the cycles of $\pi_1$ are disjoint), and its triple is $T(v)$. Since distinct blocks have distinct heads, $v\mapsto v$ is a bijection between the unblocked heads and the chains of $\mathcal M_k(u,w)$ not moving $k+1$, preserving the triples. Symmetrically, the unblocked tails $v_0$ (for which $c=1$ and $u(k+1)=v_0(k+1)$) correspond bijectively to the chains $u\tom{k+1}v\tom{k}w$ not moving $k+1$, with $S(v_0)=\big(q,\fx_{k+1}(u,v_0),\fx_k(v_0,w)\big)$. Cancelling these two families gives the equivalence with \eqref{eq:junction}. The remaining assertions follow from Proposition \ref{prop:giambelliform}.
\end{proof}

The identity \eqref{eq:junction} concerns only the \emph{junctions} of the blocks: a blocked head $v_{i_{\max}}$, whose next cut is obstructed by a cycle $e$ of $\rho_1$ with top $B\geq k+2$ through $x_{i_{\max}+1}$, and a blocked tail $v_{i_{\min}}$, obstructed by a cycle of $\rho_2$ through $x_{i_{\min}}$, together with the mixed chains in which the position $k+1$ is moved by a cycle with top $B\geq k+2$. The correspondence realising \eqref{eq:junction} \emph{transports} the obstructing cycle $e$ across $X$: in the simplest case a blocked tail with block data $(\rho_1,X,\rho_2)$ and $e\subseteq\rho_2$ through $x_j$, $j=i_{\min}$, is matched with the blocked head of the block with data $(\rho_1\cdot XeX^{-1},X,\rho_2e^{-1})$, in which $e$ has been conjugated into the first step and is attached to $x_{j-1}$, so that the cut $j-1$ is skipped; in general pivots of $X$ migrate into the transported cycle or out of it, exactly as in the transposition words $F_m$ of Lemma \ref{lemma:updown}, and a chain of $\mathcal M_k^{\mathrm{mov}}$ is matched in the same way with the head obtained by transporting the cycle through $k+1$ into the first step. The simplest junction, in which the obstructing cycle is a single transposition $t_{k+1,B}$, is exactly Lemma \ref{lemma:updown} (applied with $k+1,B$ in the roles of $k,B$): its transposition words $F_{\mathrm{up}}$, $F_{\mathrm{std}}$ and $F_m$ are a chain of $\mathcal M_k^{\mathrm{mov}}$, a chain of $\mathcal M'^{\,\mathrm{mov}}_k$ with trivial second step, and the blocked heads with block data $\big((x_n,\ldots,x_m,B),(x_m,\ldots,x_1,k+1),1\big)$, and they reappear below as the junction word $J=F_{\mathrm{up}}$ and its resolutions $J_0=F_{\mathrm{std}}$, $J_m\sim F_m$.

\subsection*{Proof of the junction identity}

We now prove \eqref{eq:junction}, and hence \eqref{eq:Bk} and Theorem \ref{theorem:doubledpieri}, for all $k$. Throughout we write $K=k+1$. The proof constructs, for every $u,w$ and every $q$-monomial $q$, an injective map from the \emph{sources} (the blocked tails and the chains of $\mathcal M_k^{\mathrm{mov}}$ of weight $q$) to the \emph{targets} (the blocked heads and the chains of $\mathcal M'^{\,\mathrm{mov}}_k$ of weight $q$) which preserves the triples, and a symmetry which exchanges sources and targets; the two together give \eqref{eq:junction}. The map is a sequence of local rewritings of the pair of chains, each governed by a single lemma (Lemma \ref{lemma:token}) which generalizes Lemma \ref{lemma:updown}; the lemma is proved not by tracking covers, but by comparing the formal $q$-weights of transposition words.

\subsubsection*{Overview}
For orientation we summarize the four kinds of configurations in \eqref{eq:junction} and the triples $(q,A,B)$, $A,B\subseteq\{1,2,\ldots\}$ sets of values, that they carry; here $M_1$, $M_2$ denote the sets of positions $\leq k$ moved by the first and by the second step, $\overline S=[1,k]\setminus S$, and $q$ is the product of the $q$-weights of the two steps.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{llll}
configuration & steps & role & triple\\\hline
blocked tail $v$ & $u\tom kv\tom kw$ & source & $S(v)=\big(q,\ u(\overline{M_1})\cup\{v(K)\},\ w(\overline{M_2})\big)$\\
chain of $\mathcal M_k^{\mathrm{mov}}$ & $u\tom kv\tom{k+1}w$, $K$ moved by step 2 & source & $\big(q,\ u(\overline{M_1}),\ w(\overline{M_2})\big)$\\
blocked head $v$ & $u\tom kv\tom kw$ & target & $T(v)=\big(q,\ u(\overline{M_1}),\ w(\overline{M_2})\cup\{v(K)\}\big)$\\
chain of $\mathcal M'^{\,\mathrm{mov}}_k$ & $u\tom{k+1}v\tom kw$, $K$ moved by step 1 & target & $\big(q,\ u(\overline{M_1}),\ w(\overline{M_2})\big)$
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
(For a blocked tail, $v(K)=u(x)$ where $x\leq k$ is the position whose next cut is obstructed, so $u(\overline{M_1})\cup\{v(K)\}=u(\overline{M_1\setminus\{x\}})$; for a blocked head, $v(K)=w(x_g)$ for the obstructed position $x_g$, so the last entry is $w(\overline{M_2\setminus\{x_g\}})$. In the mixed chains one of the two fixed sets is taken at level $k+1$, which accounts for the missing extra element.) In every case the triple is thus determined by the weight and by the two sets of positions $\leq k$ moved by the two steps, with the obstructed position removed from the appropriate set. The junction move of Proposition \ref{prop:process} rewrites a source as a transposition word with a cut and transports the obstructing cycle from the second step to the first across the top-$K$ letters; it preserves the product $u^{-1}w$, the weight (Lemma \ref{lemma:token}), and the two sets of moved positions up to the obstructed one (the invariants $N_1$ and $M_2$ below), and therefore the triple. After finitely many moves the result is a target, and the symmetry $v\mapsto w_0v$ of Lemma \ref{lemma:reversal}, which exchanges sources and targets, gives the reverse inclusion. A complete instance is worked out in Example \ref{example:junction}.

\subsubsection*{Transposition words, cover paths and formal weights}
A \emph{transposition word} is a finite sequence $W=(t_1,\ldots,t_N)$ of transpositions $t_i=t_{a_ib_i}$, $a_i<b_i$ (not of simple reflections: the pivot words and junction words below are transposition words, and the only words in the simple reflections used in this paper are the reduced words of Section \ref{section:proofvside}); its product is $t_1\cdots t_N$ and its length is $N$. Given a permutation $\psi$ put $\psi_0=\psi$ and $\psi_i=\psi_{i-1}t_i$. The $i$-th letter \emph{exchanges} the values $\psi_{i-1}(a_i)$ and $\psi_{i-1}(b_i)$, and we call it a \emph{descent letter} (from $\psi$) if $\psi_{i-1}(a_i)>\psi_{i-1}(b_i)$. The \emph{formal weight} of $W$ from $\psi$ is
$$\mathrm{wt}_\psi(W)=\prod_{i\text{ a descent letter}}q_{a_ib_i},$$
and $W$ is a \emph{cover path} from $\psi$ if $\psi_{i-1}\lup\psi_i$ for every $i$; in that case $\mathrm{wt}_\psi(W)=\prod_i\kappa(\psi_{i-1},t_i)$. Two transposition words are \emph{equivalent} if they have the same product and the same length.

\begin{lemma}[Degree criterion] \label{lemma:degree}
Let $W$ be a transposition word of length $N$ with product $\pi$. Then $\ell(\psi\pi)-\ell(\psi)\geq N-\deg\mathrm{wt}_\psi(W)$, with equality if and only if $W$ is a cover path from $\psi$. Consequently, if $W$ is a cover path from $\psi$ and $W'$ is equivalent to $W$, then $W'$ is a cover path from $\psi$ if and only if $\deg\mathrm{wt}_\psi(W')=\deg\mathrm{wt}_\psi(W)$; in particular this is the case whenever $\mathrm{wt}_\psi(W')=\mathrm{wt}_\psi(W)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By Lemma \ref{lemma:lengthchange}, the $i$-th step changes the length by at least $1$ if the letter is not a descent letter and by at least $1-2(b_i-a_i)=1-\deg q_{a_ib_i}$ if it is, with equality in both cases exactly when $\psi_{i-1}\lup\psi_i$. Summing over $i$ gives the inequality and the characterization of equality, and the rest follows because $\ell(\psi\pi)-\ell(\psi)$ and $N$ are the same for $W$ and $W'$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Inert reordering] \label{lemma:inert}
Let $W=W_1UVW_2$ be a concatenation of transposition words such that no position is moved both by a letter of $U$ and by a letter of $V$.
